A varifold-type estimation for data sampled on a rectifiable set Thanks: B. Buet acknowledges support from the French National Research Agency (ANR) under grant ANR-21-CE40-0013-01 (project GeMfaceT)
Charly Boricaud, Blanche Buet

Abstract
. We investigate the inference of varifold structures in a statistical framework: assuming that we have access to i.i.d. samples in ℝ n \mathbb{R}^{n} obtained from an underlying d d –dimensional shape S S endowed with a possibly non uniform density θ \theta , we propose and analyse an estimator of the varifold structure associated to S S . The shape S S is assumed to be piecewise C 1 , a {\rm C}^{1,a} in a sense that allows for a singular set whose small enlargements are of small d d –dimensional measure. The estimators are kernel–based both for inferring the density and the tangent spaces and the convergence result holds for the bounded Lipschitz distance between varifolds, in expectation and in a noiseless model. The mean convergence rate involves the dimension d d of S S , its regularity through a ∈ ( 0 , 1 ] a\in(0,1] and the regularity of the density θ \theta .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
49Q15; 62G05

1.
Introduction

The so called ”Manifold Hypothesis” postulates that a wide range of possibly high-dimensional data sets actually lie on a low-dimensional manifold. One can then think of point cloud data in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as one or several instances of a sampling
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
of a
d
–dimensional submanifold
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. A widely investigated question is then to
infer
(recover) information about
S
, topological and geometric features for instance, from the sample.
More precisely, we assume that there exists an underlying ”regular” object
S
given through a probability measure
\mu
carried by
S
and we furthermore assume that our data are obtained by sampling
\mu
with
N
points:
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})\sim\mu
is an i.i.d. sample and our data are an instance of the empirical measure

\mu_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{i}}\>.

The specific and important case where
S
is a
d
–dimensional submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mu
is the volume form of
S
(possibly weighted by some density) has been investigated with varying degrees of formality, different assumptions on the regularity of the manifold and for various choices of metric on shapes (Hausdorff distance, Wasserstein distances, Bounded Lipschitz distance e.g.)
[
GTS15
,
AL18
,
AL19
,
Tin23
,
Div21
,
Div22
,
TY23
,
SAL23
]
.

Let us recall that the weak convergence of
\mu_{N}
towards
\mu
holds with probability
1
for very general
S
and
\mu
(far beyond our Euclidean scope): it is known as the Glivenko-Cantelli theorem.
Moreover, in the fundamental work
[
Dud69
]
, such a weak convergence is quantified in terms of Bounded Lipschitz distance
\beta(\mu,\mu_{N})
and Prokhorov distance.
In
[
GTS15
,
GTGHS20
]
, the authors estimate the convergence of
\mu_{N}
in terms of the
\infty
–optimal transportation distance which is stronger than the aforementioned distance, and thus under the stronger assumptions that
S
is a
d
–submanifold with curvature bounds.
In such a context, one can devise and analyse estimators of geometric quantities such as tangent space and second fundamental form, as well as estimators of
S
itself. A very complete analysis is carried out in
[
AL19
]
: the authors establish general minimax bounds for the aforementioned estimators, with rates of convergence involving the size of the sample
N
, the dimension of the manifold
d
and its order of regularity. They also evidence the need of a global assumption: in addition to
{\rm C}^{k}
–regularity, one has to work with submanifolds
S
sharing a uniform lower bound on their reach to obtain convergence of the geometric estimators in strong enough topology (pointwise convergence in
S
concerning tangent spaces or second fundamental form and Hausdorff distance for estimating
S
itself). While the
{\rm C}^{k}
–manifold (
k\geq 2
) setting has been well–investigated, handling lower regularity frameworks is a natural next issue. Lower regularity may be thought in terms of lowering local parametric regularity, relaxing global structural assumptions such as reach bounds and even questionning the global manifold assumption allowing immersions, more general singular sets as well as multi–dimensional models as in the recent work
[
AB24
]
.

In the present work, we consider the problem at hand from a measure perspective: our main purpose is to infer the so-called
varifold
structure
V_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{T_{x}S}
associated with
S
(see Definition
2.15
). The varifold
V_{S}
can be defined for a smooth
d
–submanifold
S
but also for less regular objects such as
d
–rectifiable sets, and it encodes the order
1
structure of
S
: for a
d
–submanifold
S
,
V_{S}
is a measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}
whose support is
\{(x,T_{x}S)\>:\>x\in S\}
. Let us describe the concrete differences that come along with such a change of standpoint. First, while the manifold setting naturally allows to consider the tangent space as a continuous function in
S
, we only have a weaker counterpart in the varifold setting and we consequently do not expect that an associated estimator converge pointwise in
S
but rather as measures or almost everywhere in
S
. Similarly, we do not wish to estimate
S
for the Hausdorff distance but rather to estimate the
d
–dimensional Hausdorff measure
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
carried by
S
and then the varifold structure
V_{S}
both for the so called
Bounded Lipschitz distance

\beta
, which is related to weak convergence of measures.
Coming along with the measure perspective is the issue of the possible non uniformity of
\mu
. Indeed,
depending on the nature of the collected data, it is not adapted to assume that
\mu
is well distributed in
S
: such lack of uniformity can be modelled assuming that
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
for some positive density function
\theta
uniformly lower and upper bounded in
S
:
0<\theta_{min}\leq\theta\leq\theta_{max}<\infty
.
Such an assumption is common in the literature, however, the usual issue that is addressed is the estimation of
\theta
and
\mu
, whereas we are interested in estimating
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}=\frac{1}{\theta}\mu
(similarly to what is considered in
[
Div22
]
in a manifold framework), which is a closely related yet slightly different question.
In such a case, it is important to decouple the geometric information contained in
S
from the whole information encoded by
\mu
and we naturally adopt a two–step approach, we first estimate
\theta
and then we design an estimator for
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
. Once the reconstruction of
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
has been carried out, we can tackle the varifold estimation. Again, we first analyse the a.e. pointwise convergence of a tangent space estimator under rectifiability assumption and we then move to the issue of estimating the whole varifold structure
V_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{T_{x}S}
. Let us describe more precisely both the density and the tangent estimation leading to the reconstruction of the varifold structure.

1.1.
Reconstruction of the varifold structure

Density and measure estimation.

Note that if we were considering the case of a probability measure
\mu=\theta\mathcal{L}^{n}
which is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure
\mathcal{L}^{n}
, we could rely on Lebesgue differentiation theorem with respect to balls: for a.e.
x
,

\theta(x)=\lim_{\delta\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mu(B(x,\delta))}{\mathcal{L}^{n}(B(x,\delta))}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{L}^{n}(B(0,1))}\lim_{\delta\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mu(B(x,\delta))}{\delta^{d}}\>,

in order to estimate
\theta
. There is no equivalent property applicable to any Radon measure
\mu
absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathcal{H}^{d}
, however, assuming
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
for a
d
–rectifiable
set
S
is enough to similarly obtain for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a.e.
x\in S
,

\theta(x)=\frac{1}{\mathcal{L}^{d}(B(0,1))}\lim_{\delta\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mu(B(x,\delta))}{\delta^{d}}\quad\text{and also}\quad\theta(x)=\frac{1}{C_{\eta}}\lim_{\delta\to 0_{+}}\frac{\int_{B(x,\delta)}\eta\left(\frac{|y|}{\delta}\right)\>d\mu(y)}{\delta^{d}}=\frac{1}{C_{\eta}}\lim_{\delta\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mu\ast\eta_{\delta}(x)}{\delta^{d}}

for a smooth radial profile
\eta
.
Such an observation leads to consider a usual kernel-based approximate density (see (
19
))

\theta_{\delta}(x)=(C_{\eta}\delta^{d})^{-1}\mu\ast\eta_{\delta}
associated with the natural estimator
\theta_{\delta,N}=(C_{\eta}\delta^{d})^{-1}\mu_{N}\ast\eta_{\delta}
. We recall in Proposition
3.5
the control of the fluctuation
\mathbb{E}\left[|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta}(x)|\right]
(see
[
BH21
]
). Regarding the convergence of
\theta_{\delta}
towards
\theta
, it holds almost everywhere in
S
as soon as
S
is
d
–rectifiable, however, its quantification requires to strengthen the regularity assumptions and leads to Proposition
7.5
.
Once
\theta
has been estimated, we propose an estimator
\nu_{\delta,N}\simeq\frac{1}{\theta_{\delta,N}}\mu_{N}
that converges to
\frac{1}{\theta}\mu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
in terms of the Bounded Lipschitz distance
\beta
under rectifiability assumptions (see Theorem
4.9
, Corollary
4.10
) though again not in a minimax sense that also requires to strengthen the regularity class at hand as done in Theorem
7.4
.

Tangent space and varifold estimation.

The very practical problem of estimating tangent spaces is a long-standing question that has already been substantially addressed. However, there are few theoretical guarantees when the set
S
is only assumed to be rectifiable.
Relying on the Principal Component Analysis approach,
we directly consider the
\varphi
–weighted (and properly renormalized) covariance matrix
\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)
in order to approximate
T_{x}S
with scale parameter
r>0
:

\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\int_{B(x,r)}\varphi\left(\frac{y-x}{r}\right)\frac{y-x}{r}\otimes\frac{y-x}{r}\>d\mu(y)\>.

We point out that in
[
Tin23
]
, such a tangent plane estimator
\Sigma_{r}
has proven to be efficient in the reconstruction of the varifold structure associated with an immersed manifold
S
, obtaining explicit convergence rate for the
p
–Wasserstein distance between the exact and the reconstructed varifolds. Our purpose however differs both because of the weaker regularity we assume and because we do not consider the deterministic reconstruction issue (as done in
[
Tin23
]
) but the mean convergence reconstruction of the varifold structure. We first analyse the pointwise reconstruction of the approximate tangent space in Propositions
5.3
and
5.6
,
the reconstruction of the whole varifold then requires to put density and tangent estimation together as done in Proposition
5.8
. Once again, one has to strengthen the regularity assumptions to obtain “minimax convergence” (we do not mean minimax optimal but uniform in some regularity class as explained below) for both tangent space and varifold structure estimators as done in Theorem
7.4
.
It is a very important stage to reach since higher order estimators will rely on the quality of the approximation of the varifold structure. More specifically, we believe that inferring the varifold structure would allow to infer curvature information thanks to the approximations that have been proposed in
[
BLM17
]
and
[
BLM22
]
.

Piecewise Hölder regularity class and main result.

As mentioned above, the convergence results stated in Sections
3
–
4
–
5
do not fit a minimax convergence setting. Let us be more explicit: let

\mathcal{Q}=\{\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\>:\>S,\>\theta\text{ satisfy \hyperref@@ii[hypH1]{$(H_{1})$}--\hyperref@@ii[hypH2]{$(H_{2})$}--\hyperref@@ii[hypH3]{$(H_{3})$}}\}

be a regularity class of measures depending on
d,C_{0},\theta_{min/max}
. In short,
\mu\in\mathcal{Q}
if it is
d
–Ahlfors regular with constant
C_{0}
,
d
–rectifiable and the density
0<\theta_{min}\leq\theta\leq\theta_{max}<\infty
is upper and lower bounded. Let
\hat{\vartheta}_{N}=\hat{\vartheta}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
be an estimator of some quantity
\vartheta(\mu)
depending of
\mu
.
A convergence result for
\hat{\vartheta}
fits the minimax setting in the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
if the convergence is uniform with respect to the class
\mathcal{Q}
:

\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\hat{\vartheta}_{N},\vartheta(\mu))\right]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0\>,

for some loss
\ell
between
\hat{\vartheta}_{N}
and
\vartheta
. A natural question is then to find the best estimators possible and investigate both (asymptotic
N\to\infty
) lower and upper bounds for the so-called minimax risk that consider all possible estimators
\hat{\vartheta}_{N}
:

\mathcal{R}_{N}(\mathcal{Q})=\inf_{\hat{\vartheta}_{N}}\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\hat{\vartheta}_{N},\vartheta(\mu))\right]\>.

An estimator
\hat{\vartheta}_{N}
is
minimax optimal
over
\mathcal{Q}
if its asymptotical convergence rate is
\mathcal{R}_{N}(\mathcal{Q})
up to a constant.
In Sections
3
–
4
–
5
, the convergence results we obtain do not fit the minimax setting since they are not uniform over the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
. More precisely, we are only able to prove results of the form: for a given
\mu\in\mathcal{Q}
and adapting some parameter (denoted by
\delta_{N}
thereafter) we have
\mathbb{E}\left[\ell(\hat{\vartheta}_{N},\vartheta(\mu))\right]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0
. In Sections
6
and
7
, we consequently strengthen the assumptions on
\mu
and consider instead of
\mathcal{Q}
, the regularity class:

\mathcal{P}=\{\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\>:\>S,\>\theta\text{ satisfy \hyperref@@ii[hypH1]{$(H_{1})$} to \eqref{hypH7}}\}

that now depends on
d,\widetilde{C_{0}},\theta_{min/max}
and
C=\max(C_{\theta,sg},C_{S,sg}),R=\min(R_{\theta,sg},R_{S,sg})
as introduced in Definitions
6.2
and
6.5
. Loosely speaking, we consider in
\mathcal{P}
measures of the form
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
for which
\theta
and
S
are uniformly piecewise respectively
{\rm C}^{0,b}
and
{\rm C}^{1,a}
up to a singular set
\mathfrak{S}
of zero
\mu
–measure, and we assume moreover that
\mathfrak{S}
has co-dimension at least
1
in
S
in the sense that
\mathfrak{S}
is a union of
l
–Ahlfors regular sets for
0\leq l\leq d-1
. We therefore in particular allow for
S
to be a smooth manifold with boundary or to be piecewise smooth up to auto-crossings and junctions along a set
\mathfrak{S}
that is reasonably small in the aforementioned sense. We introduce and investigate the convergence of estimators in the regularity class
\mathcal{P}
that we refer to as the
piecewise Hölder regularity class
. The main result we obtain is Theorem
7.4
that establishes a convergence result for an estimator
\widehat{V}_{N}
of the varifold structure
W_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{\Pi_{T_{x}S}}
(see (
6
)) that is uniform with respect to the
\mu\in\mathcal{P}
(with
S=\supp\mu
) when the loss
\ell=\beta
is the so-called
Bounded Lipschitz distance
(Definition
2.3
):

\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[\beta(\widehat{V}_{N},W_{S})\right]\leq M\>N^{-\frac{\min(a,b)}{d+2\min(a,b)}}

where the constant
M
only depends on
d,n,\widetilde{C_{0}},\theta_{min/max},C=\max(C_{\theta,sg},C_{S,sg}),R=\min(R_{\theta,sg},R_{S,sg})
that are the parameters of the regularity class
\mathcal{P}
.
In other words, we obtain an explicit upper bound of the minimax risk
\mathcal{R}_{N}(\mathcal{P})
concerning the inference of the varifold structure with respect to the Bounded Lipschitz distance
\beta
.
According to us, a very important point achieved with such a result is the following: it proves that it is possible to obtain minimax convergence results for reconstructing the order
1
information of
S=\supp\mu
despite the presence of a singular set, hence beyond the manifold hypothesis. A crucial point is to consider a loss, in our case
\ell=\beta
, associated with a topology weaker than a pointwise loss.

Let us now comment on some achievements, limitations and perspectives of our study.

1.2.
Comments and perspectives

Reach assumption.

In addition to local smoothness requirements, it has been evidenced in the literature the necessity of global regularity assumptions in order to obtain minimax inference results in at least
{\rm C}^{2}
manifolds models, we refer to
[
AL19
]
as well as
[
BH21
]
(Thm 2.6 in Section 2.5) in the case of the pointwise density inference where the necessity of lower bounding the
reach
of the manifold is established.
Let us recall that the reach of a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined as

{\rm reach}(K)=\sup\{r\geq 0\>|\>\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},d(x,K)\leq r\implies\exists!y\in K,\,d(x,K)=|x-y|\}\>.

Assuming that
{\rm reach}(S)\geq\kappa>0
for instance ensures that
S
does not have auto-intersection in a quantative way:
S
has a
\kappa
–neighborhood in which no piece of
S
that would be far in terms of geodesic distance can go through.
Let us comment on the fact that our results are however obtained without explicitly requiring any lower bound on the
reach
of
S
.
First of all, as already mentioned above, the results of Sections
3
,
4
and
5
do not meet the usual minimax framework: indeed,
we obtain convergence results of the following type, for instance considering the density estimator
\theta_{\delta_{N},N}
: one can chose
\delta_{N}\to 0
such that for each
\mu\in\mathcal{Q}
and for
\mu
–a.e.
x
,

(1)

\mathbb{E}\left[|\theta_{\delta_{N},N}(x)-\theta(x)|\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>,

but the convergence holds without any uniform bounds. This first observation explain the absence of lower bounded reach assumption in those three Sections
3
,
4
and
5
. However, in Sections
6
and
7
, we obtain uniform convergence results with respect to the piecewise Hölder regularity class
\mathcal{P}
as stated in Theorem
7.4
and Proposition
7.5
.
Our understanding is that such a result illustrates that the necessity of lower bounding the reach holds when establishing pointwise convergence results but not for “weaker” convergence like the Bounded Lipschitz or the
{\rm L}^{1}
convergence (see also (
7.5
)), for which it is sufficient to control the measure of the bad set
\mathfrak{S}
in some quantitative way. We believe that it is an interesting starting point in finding a consistent inference model allowing for low-regularity and singularities in
S
, beyond the
{\rm C}^{2}
–manifold model.
We also obtain a pointwise result assuming that the singular set
\mathfrak{S}
is empty (see (
178
) in Proposition
7.5
) in which we recover known minimax pointwise rate for the density
\theta
of order
N^{-\frac{\min(a,b)}{d+2\min(a,b)}}
, see
[
BH21
]
. Note that in this particular case, though we do not explicitly assume any lower bound on the reach, Definition
6.2
however requires that
S
is a
d
–manifold (only in this particular case where
\mathfrak{S}=\emptyset
) and furthermore a
{\rm C}^{1,a}
graph in any ball of radius less than
R
with uniform Hölder constant. On one hand, such assumptions prevent that parts of
S
could get arbitrarily close in the ambient space while distant in the instrinsic metric of
S
(induced by the ambient one) similarly to what a reach bound would ensure. On the other hand, we also emphasize that we assume that the density
\theta
is Hölder with respect to the ambient metric (see Definition
6.5
), which is different from the model investigated in
[
BH21
]
where the Hölder regularity of the density is assumed in terms of geodesic distance in
S
, which makes sense since
S
is a regular manifold in their work.

Minimax lower bound and optimal rates.

To the best of our knowledge, there is no existing result to infer the varifold structure and more generally, there are few inference results in such a low regularity framework. In particular, lower bounds for the minimax rates are yet to established. Nonetheless, we first point out that our analysis provides the same rate for the estimation of
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
as for the estimation of the varifold
W_{S}
(see Theorem
7.4
), yet,
W_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}\otimes\delta_{\Pi_{T_{x}S}}
involves the order
1
structure: we expect that
\nu
can be inferred with a better rate than
W_{S}
. Note that in the case where
S
is at least a
{\rm C}^{2}
manifold with lower bounded reach,

[
Div22
]
establishes minimax rates concerning the inference of
\nu
in terms of Wasserstein distance, assuming Besov regularity
B_{p,q}^{b}(S)
for the density and similar lower and upper bound
\theta_{min/max}
. In particular, for
d\geq 3
, they establish a minimax rate of order
N^{-\frac{1+b}{d+2b}}
that is, at least loosely speaking, of order
\sim\delta_{N}N^{-\frac{\min(a,b)}{d+2\min(a,b)}}
and we observe a gain of the factor
\delta_{N}
when compared to the rate we obtain in Theorem
7.4
. From a technical point of view, a key point in their proof to achieve such a rate is to perform the estimation of
\theta
in Sobolev
{\rm H}^{-1}(S)
–norm rather than pointwisely. Unfortunately, the counterpart of this approach is not clear in our regularity framework.
As for the estimation of the order
1
structure, we are not aware of results beyond (uniform) pointwise estimator of the tangent space. In
[
AL19
]
, a
{\rm C}^{k}
–manifold regularity model is investigated and concerning the pointwise estimation of tangent spaces, a lower bound for the minimax risk of order
N^{\frac{k-1}{d}}
is established (in the noise free model), and an almost optimal (up to a logarithmic factor) estimator is given. Formally replacing
k
with
1+a
in our regularity class, we can compare our convergence rate
N^{\frac{\min(a,b)}{d+2\min(a,b)}}
with
N^{\frac{k-1}{d}}\sim N^{\frac{a}{d}}
that indicates some room for improvement if the known
{\rm C}^{k}
–manifolds minimax rates consistently extend for
1<k<2
, which is yet another point that emphasizes the importance of searching for minimax lower bounds for low regularity models. We note that recent results establish minimax rates of order
N^{\frac{a+\gamma}{d+2a}}
(
1\leq\gamma\leq a+1
) for the estimation of
\nu
(though not
\mu
up to our understanding) with respect to a distance
d_{\mathcal{H}^{\gamma}}
similar to the bounded Lipschitz distance but adding a Hölder condition on the first order derivative of test functions. Such minimax rates are valid in a manifold framework and it would be worth understanding wether such manifold regularity can be relaxed to establish minimax rates in a piecewise manifold regularity framework similar to the one we investigate.

Impact of noise and higher order structure inference.

We restricted our setting to an idealistic noise free estimation to carry out the analysis of a varifold estimator. The natural and important next step is to include noise, for instance through an additive noise model as suggested in
[
AL18
]
(for the estimation of
S
through a simplicial complex and in terms of Hausdorff distance). It would also be important to understand the respective impacts of tangential and normal component of such an additive noise: loosely speaking, we expect that the tangential perturbations are connected to the uniformity of the sampling while the normal perturbations directly affect the geometry of
S
. To this end, it is for instance possible to explore a “tubular noise” model, as already done in
[
AL19
]
for the
{\rm C}^{k}
–model.
An important motivation to carry out the analysis of such kernel-based estimators in a low regularity framework roots in
[
BLM17
,
BLM22
]
where the authors introduce approximations of mean curvature and more generally second fundamental form based on varifolds theory. They show the convergence of such approximations and some partial stability involving localized Bounded Lipschitz distances under low regularity assumptions compatible with the piecewise Hölder regularity class
\mathcal{P}
introduced
in Section
6
. However, in
[
BLM17
,
BLM22
]
, the deterministic setting is a limitation to obtain more tractable convergence rates and we hope that transferring the issue into a statistical inference setting would lead to more explicit bounds. We expect that in the case of the mean curvature vector estimation, tangential noise would produce tangential error, and the mean curvature vector being normal to
S
, it is then possible to deal with such error as long as we have a good estimation of the tangent space: concretely projecting onto the normal space, as implemented in
[
BLM17
]
. In this particular case at least, it is relevant to consider noise model in which it is possible to split tangential and normal components.

Unknown parameters of the estimators.

We finally underline that though uniform in the regularity class
\mathcal{P}
:

(1)

The parameter
\tau
used to define the estimators (see (
56
), Proposition
4.8
and Remark
4.11
) depends on
d
,
C_{0}
and
\eta
. Though
\eta
is known, and
d
might be known in some cases,
C_{0}
has to be estimated.

(2)

The definitions of our estimators also rely on the choice of the parameter
\delta_{N}
of order
N^{\frac{1}{d+2\min(a,b)}}
, and
a
and
b
are not known either in general.

1.3.
Organisation of the paper

The paper can be divided into
3
parts: Sections
2
and
3
are preliminary sections. Sections
4
and
5
investigate the varifold inference issue in the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
(
9
) in which convergence comes without uniform bounds, see Corollary
5.11
. Sections
6
and
7
then address the issue in the class
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{Q}
(
100
): strengthening the regularity setting allows to obtain uniform convergence as stated in the main result Theorem
7.4
.

Notations

We fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
n\geq 1
and
d\in\mathbb{R}
,
0<d\leq n
.

•

In Sections
2
to
4
,
d
is real, unless otherwise specified. In Sections
5
to
7
,
d
is an integer. Generally speaking, rectifiability and
d
–varifold structure require
d
to be an integer, while
d
–Ahlfors regularity is well-defined for
d
real.

•

We use a generic positive constant
M
whose default dependency is given at the beginning of each sections (in Remark
5.1
,
6.6
and
7.2
).

•

Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r>0
, we set
B(x,r)=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\,|\,|y-x|<r\right\}
.

•

For
\delta>0,
the open
\delta
-neighbourhood (
\delta
-thickening) of
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is

(2)

A^{\delta}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:d(x,A)<\delta\}=\bigcup\limits_{x\in A}B(x,\delta)\>.

•

\mathcal{L}^{n}
is the
n
–dimensional Lebesgue measure and
\omega_{n}=\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{1}(0))
.

•

\mathcal{H}^{d}
is the
d
–dimensional Hausdorff measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

{\rm M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is the space of square matrices with real entries and of size
n
. We consider
\|\cdot\|
the matrix (operator) norm associated with the euclidean norm in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

{\rm Sym}(n)\subset{\rm M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is the subspace of symmetric matrices and
{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)\subset{\rm Sym}(n)
is the set of positive semi-definite matrices.

•

{\rm P}_{d,n}=\left\{A\in{\rm Sym}(n)\>:\>A^{2}=A\text{ and }\trace A=d\right\}
is the compact subset of rank–
d
orthogonal projectors.

•

{\rm G}_{d,n}
denotes the Grassmannian of
d
-dimensional vector subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

{\rm G}_{d,n}=\{d\text{--dimensional vector subspace of }\mathbb{R}^{n}\}\>.

A
d
-dimensional subspace
T
will be often identified with the orthogonal projection onto
T
, denoted as
\Pi_{T}\in{\rm M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
.
{\rm G}_{d,n}
is equipped with the metric
d(T,P)=\|\Pi_{T}-\Pi_{P}\|
so that

\begin{array}[]{rccl}i:&{\rm G}_{d,n}&\rightarrow&{\rm P}_{d,n}\\
&T&\mapsto&\Pi_{T}\end{array}\quad\text{is a bijective isometry.}

•

{\rm C}_{c}(X)
is the space of continuous real–valued and compactly supported functions defined on a topological space
X

•

{\rm Lip}(X)
is the space of real–valued Lipschitz functions
f
defined on a metric space
(X,\delta)
with Lipschitz constant
{\rm Lip}(f)
.

•

Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\geq 1
,
{\rm C}_{c}^{k}(\Omega)
is the space of real–valued functions of class
{\rm C}^{k}
with compact support in the open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

•

The constant
C_{0}\geq 1
(respectively
\widetilde{C_{0}}\geq 1
) is a Ahlfors regularity constant for
\mu
(respectively
\nu
), see Definition
2.5
.

2.
Preliminary notions around rectifiability and varifolds

We recall some elementary facts concerning Radon measures, Ahlfors regularity, rectifiability and varifolds. We restrict ourselves to what is necessary to the understanding of the paper and we refer to
[
Mat95
]
,
[
EG91
]
,
[
AFP00
]
and
[
Sim83
]
for more details.

2.1.
Radon measures and Ahlfors regularity

We restrict ourselves to the case of Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}
or
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
, whence
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}
or
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
hereafter.

Definition 2.1

(Radon measure)
.

We say that
\mu
is a Radon measure on
X
if
\mu
is a locally finite Borel measure.

We know from Riesz representation theorem that Radon measures can alternatively be defined as continuous linear form on
{\rm C}_{c}(X)
, leading to the so called
weak star
convergence of Radon measures.

Definition 2.2

(Weak star convergence of Radon measures)
.

Let
\mu,(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be Radon measures on
X
. We say that
(\mu_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
weak star converges to
\mu
, and we write
\mu_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\mu
if

\forall f\in{\rm C}_{c}(X),\quad\int_{X}f\>d\mu_{k}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\int_{X}f\>d\mu\>.

We also introduced the following distance among Radon measures, that sometimes is referred to as flat distance in the context of varifolds.

Definition 2.3

(Bounded Lipschitz distance)
.

Let
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
be two Radon measures in
X
, then

\beta(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\sup\left\{\left|\int_{X}f\>d\lambda_{1}-\int_{X}f\>d\lambda_{2}\right|:f\in{\rm C}_{c}(X,\mathbb{R}),\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1,{\rm Lip}(f)\leq 1\right\}

defines a distance in the space of Radon measures.

We will also consider a localized version that allows to compare measures in a given open set
D
:

Definition 2.4

(Localized Bounded Lipschitz distance)
.

Let
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}
be Radon measures in
X
and let
D\subset X
be an open set, we consider

\beta_{D}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=\sup\left\{\left|\int_{X}f\>d\lambda_{1}-\int_{X}f\>d\lambda_{2}\right|:f\in{{\rm C}}_{c}(X,\mathbb{R}),\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1,{{\rm Lip}}(f)\leq 1,\supp f\subset D\right\}.

In the case where
X=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
, we only localize with respect to the spatial part, that is considering
\beta_{D\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)}
that we abusively denote by
\beta_{D}
.

Such a localized version is symmetric and satisfies the triangular inequality, yet note that
\beta_{D}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=0
only implies that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}
in the open set
D
thus defining a distance in the space of Radon measures in
D
.

The following notion of
d
–Ahlfors regularity for a measure
\mu
requires the measure
\mu(B(x,r))
of balls centered at
x\in\supp\mu
to be comparable with the
d
–volume
\omega_{d}r^{d}
of such a ball.

Definition 2.5

(Ahlfors regularity)
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for
d>0
, we say that
\mu
is
d
–Ahlfors regular (or simply
d
–Ahlfors) if

\exists C_{0}\geq 1
such that
\forall x\in\supp(\mu)
,
\forall r\in(0,{\rm diam}(\supp\mu)]
,

(3)

\frac{1}{C_{0}}r^{d}\leq\mu(B(x,r))\leq C_{0}r^{d}\>.

Such a constant
C_{0}\geq 1
will be referred as a Ahlfors regularity constant for
\mu
. We also say that a closed set
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is
d
–Ahlfors regular if
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|E}
is
d
–Ahlfors regular.

Remark 2.6

(Finite Ahlfors regular measure)
.

Let
\mu
be a
d
–Ahlfors regular measure in the sense of Definition
2.5
above and furthermore assume that
\mu
is finite (we will assume that
\mu
is a probability measure later in the paper). Then,

(1)

the measure
\mu
is compactly supported. Indeed, assume by contradiction that
{\rm diam}(\supp\mu)=\infty
, then for all
r>0
and for some
x\in\supp\mu
,

C_{0}^{-1}r^{d}\leq\mu(B(x,r))\leq\mu(\mathbb{R}^{n})<\infty\>,

which is impossible letting
r\to+\infty
.

(2)

It is equivalent (up to adapting
C_{0}
) to require (
3
) only for small radii
r\in(0,r_{0}]
with
0<r_{0}<R:={\rm diam}(\supp\mu)
. Indeed, let
x\in\supp\mu
then
R<\infty
thanks to
(i)
and thus, for
r_{0}<r\leq R
, one has

0<C_{0}^{-1}\frac{r_{0}^{d}}{R^{d}}\leq\frac{\mu(B(x,r_{0}))}{R^{d}}\leq\frac{\mu(B(x,r))}{r^{d}}\leq\frac{\mu(B(x,R))}{r_{0}^{d}}<+\infty\>.

Example 2.7

(
d
–Ahlfors measures.)
.

Examples of
d
–Ahlfors regular measures include Lebesgue measure on a
d
–subspace,
d
–Hausdorff measure on a smooth closed
d
–submanifold. More generally,
d
–Ahlfors regularity is preserved through bi–Lipschitz mappings so that Lipschitz graphs are Ahlfors regular. Yet, there are also less regular examples:
for instance, the
d
–Hausdorff measure on the Koch snowflake is
d
–Ahlfors regular for
d=\log_{3}4
. Even worse, the “
4
–corners Cantor
set” (see
[
DS93
]
chapter 1) obtained by dividing a unit square into
4\times 4
equally sized squares, deleting all but the
4
corners squares and iterating the process is known to be
1
–Ahlfors regular though purely–
1
–unrectifiable. Ahlfors regularity conveys information on the dimensionality of an object but is not restrictive in terms of smoothness. Another interesting example (rather counter-example) is given by the following highly oscillating curve

\mathcal{C}=\left\{\left(x,\sin\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\right)\>:\>x\in(0,1]\right\}
which is
{\rm C}^{1}
while
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{|\mathcal{C}}
is not
1
–Ahlfors regular. Note that in this example
\mathcal{C}
is not closed and its closure
\overline{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{C}\cup\{0\}\times[0,1]
is not a
{\rm C}^{1}
curve. On the other hand, the graph
\left\{\left(x,f(x)\right)\>:\>x\in K\right\}
of a
{\rm C}^{1}
function
f:K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n-d}
over a compact set
K
is
d
–Ahlfors regular (since
f
is Lipschitz in
K
).
We refer to
[
DS97
]
for additional examples and properties connected to
d
–Ahlfors regularity.

The following proposition (Proposition
2.8
) gives a bound on the number of balls with common diameter
\delta
needed to cover the support of a
d
–Ahlfors measure. Such estimates will be crucial in the proof of Proposition
4.3
. For the sake of clarity, we give the proof of Proposition
2.8
and we refer to
[
Mat95
]
(Section 5) for further details.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
S=\supp(\mu)
. Assume that there exists
d>0,C_{0}\geq 1

such that
\forall x\in S,

\forall 0<r<{\rm diam}(S)
,

(4)

\mu(B(x,r))\geq C_{0}^{-1}r^{d}\>.

Then, for all bounded set
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and for all
\delta>0
, the minimal number
m(B\cap S,\delta)
of sets of diameter smaller than
\delta
needed to form a partition of
B\cap S
satisfies

(5)

m(B\cap S,\delta)\leq 4^{d}C_{0}\delta^{-d}\mu\left(B^{\frac{\delta}{4}}\right)\>.

Remark 2.9
.

We note that (
4
) is in particular satisfied by
d
–Ahlfors measures

Proof.

Given a bounded set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\delta>0
,
m(A,\delta)
is the minimal number of sets of diameter smaller than
\delta
needed to form a partition of
A
and we additionally introduce:

(1)

the packing number
P(A,\delta)
as the greatest number of disjoint balls of radius
\delta
with center in
A
,

(2)

the smallest number of balls with radius
\delta
needed to cover
A
, denoted by
V(A,\delta)
.

Step
1
: We will prove that

m(A,\delta)\leq V\left(A,\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\leq P\left(A,\frac{\delta}{4}\right)\>.

Indeed, denoting
k=P(A,\delta)
, let
B(x_{1},\delta),\dots,B(x_{k},\delta)
be disjoint balls with centers
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}\in A
.
Suppose that
V(A,2\delta)>k
then
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}B(x_{i},2\delta)
does not cover
A
and thus there exists
x\in A\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}B(x_{i},2\delta)
.
Consequently
B(x,\delta),B(x_{1},\delta),\dots,B(x_{k},\delta)
are
k+1
disjoint balls of radius
\delta
with center in
A
, that is impossible by definition of
k
. We conclude that
V(A,2\delta)\leq P(A,\delta)
.

Let now
\displaystyle k^{\prime}=V\left(A,\frac{\delta}{2}\right)
and
B_{1},\dots,B_{k^{\prime}}
be balls of radius
\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{2}
such that
\displaystyle A\subset\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{k^{\prime}}B_{j}
.
We can take
A_{1}=A\cap B_{1}
then
A_{2}=A\cap B_{2}\setminus A_{1}
up to
A_{k^{\prime}}=(A\cap B_{k^{\prime}})\setminus{\bigcup\limits_{j=1}^{k^{\prime}-1}}A_{j}

Then
A_{1},\dots,A_{k^{\prime}}
form a partition of
A
with sets of diameter smaller than
\delta
and
m(A,\delta)\leq k^{\prime}
.

Step
2
: Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded set, then
\displaystyle P(B\cap S,\delta)\leq C_{0}\mu(B^{\delta})\delta^{-d}
.
Indeed, let
k=P(B\cap S,\delta)
and
B_{1},\dots,B_{k}
be disjoint balls of radius
\delta
and centered in
B\cap S
. Then for
i=1,\dots,k
, (
4
) gives
\mu(B_{i})\geq C_{0}^{-1}\delta^{d}
and therefore

k\>C_{0}^{-1}\delta^{d}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mu(B_{i})=\mu\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{i=1}^{k}B_{i}\right)\leq\mu(B^{\delta})\quad\Rightarrow\quad P(B\cap S,\delta)=k\leq C_{0}\mu(B^{\delta})\delta^{-d}\>.

Step
1
and Step
2
lead to (
5
).
∎

2.2.
Rectifiability

We first recall some classical definitions and results on rectifiability and we refer to
[
AFP00
]
for more details.

Definition 2.10

(
d
–rectifiable set)
.

Let
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Borel set, we say that
S
is countably
d
–rectifiable if there exists a countable family
(f_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of Lipschitz maps from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathcal{H}^{d}\left(S\setminus\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right)=0\>.

We say that
S
is
d
–rectifiable if moreover
\mathcal{H}^{d}(S)<\infty
(which will be the case hereafter).

Note that the above notion of rectifiability are referred to as (countable)
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–rectifiability in
[
AFP00
]
, Definition 2.57.
We then define (non-negative) rectifiable measures as those carried by rectifiable sets:

Definition 2.11

(Rectifiable measures,
[
AFP00
]
Definition 2.59)
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We say that
\mu
is
d
–rectifiable if there exist a countably
d
–rectifiable set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a Borel function
\theta:S\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}_{|S}^{d}
.

In other words, a
d
–rectifiable set
S
is included in a countable union of
d
–dimensional Lipschitz graphs up to a
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–negligible set. Then recalling that Lipschitz functions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
are a.e. differentiable, it is natural (to try) to define tangent planes a.e on rectifiable sets. Yet, it is crucial to have a notion that does not depend on the choice of
a covering of
S
with Lipschitz graphs:

Definition 2.12

(Approximate tangent plane,
[
AFP00
]
Definition 2.79)
.

Let
\mu
be a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We say that
\mu
has approximate tangent space (or plane)
P\in G_{d,n}
with multiplicity
\theta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
at
x
, if for all
f\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\lim\limits_{r\to 0_{+}}r^{-d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\left(\frac{y-x}{r}\right)\>d\mu(y)=\theta(x)\int_{P}f(y)d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)\>.

Proposition 2.13

(A.e. existence of approximate tangent space for rectifiable measures,
[
AFP00
]
Proposition 2.83)
.

Let
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
be a
d
–rectifiable measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a.e.
x\in S
,

(1)

\mu
admits an approximate tangent space with multiplicity
\theta(x)
, denoted by
T_{x}S
,

(2)

in particular
\displaystyle\theta(x)=\lim_{r\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mu(B(x,r))}{\omega_{d}r^{d}}
.

Note that Proposition 2.83 in
[
AFP00
]
actually characterize the rectifiability of
\mu
through the existence of approximate tangent space, but it is not necessary in this paper. Note also that such an approximate tangent space to
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
actually does not depend on
\theta
(see
[
AFP00
]
Proposition 2.85) and we will use the notation
T_{x}S
for the approximate tangent space.

2.3.
Varifolds

We introduce in this section some basic definitions concerning
d
–varifolds in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Our purpose is not to describe the formalism of varifolds in an abstract setting, for instance we do not define the class of rectifiable
d
–varifolds but we only explain how to associate a
d
–varifold
V_{S}
with a
d
–rectifiable set
S
. We refer to
[
Sim83
]
for a comprehensive reference. We also point out
[
Men17
]
for a short introduction through examples. We recall that varifolds have been introduced and investigated to tackle the existence of minimal surfaces and we refer to the seminal paper of
[
All72
]
.

First of all, note that there is a one-to-one correspondence between
{\rm G}_{d,n}
and the subset of rank–
d
orthogonal projectors
{\rm P}_{d,n}
of
{\rm M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
via
i:T\in{\rm G}_{d,n}\mapsto\Pi_{T}
, where we recall that
\Pi_{T}\in{\rm M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is the orthogonal projector onto the
d
–vector subspace
T
. We endow
{\rm G}_{d,n}
with the distance
d
satisfying for
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
in
{\rm G}_{d,n}
,
d(T_{1},T_{2})=\|\Pi_{T_{1}}-\Pi_{T_{2}}\|
, in other words, with such a metric in
{\rm G}_{d,n}
,
i
is an isometry.

Definition 2.14

(
d
–varifold in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
)
.

A
d
–varifold in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a non-negative Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}
.

Beyond such a general definition, there are natural
d
–varifolds associated with geometric objects such as
d
–submanifolds and
d
–rectifiable sets:

Definition 2.15

(Rectifiable
d
–varifold associated with a
d
–rectifiable set)
.

Let
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a
d
–rectifiable set. We can define the
rectifiable
d
–varifold

V_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{T_{x}S}
in the sense:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}}f(x,P)\,dV_{S}(x,P)=\int_{S}f(x,T_{x}S)\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(x)\quad\forall f\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n})\>,

where
T_{x}S
is the approximate tangent space at
x
which exists
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–almost everywhere in
S
.

We recall that using Riesz representation theorem, we can define
d
–varifolds as non-negative linear forms on
{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n})
, as done in Definition
2.15
above.

An important feature of varifolds is that they can model discrete geometric objects as well. In our context, we are more specifically in
point cloud varifolds
(see
[
BLM17
]
) that are associated with sets of points:

Definition 2.16

(Point cloud varifold)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and assume that we are given: a finite set of points
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1\ldots N}\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
, associated with positive weights
(m_{i})_{i=1\ldots N}
and
d
–dimensional directions
(P_{i})_{i=1\ldots N}
in
{\rm G}_{d,n}
.
We can associate with such data the
point cloud
d
–varifold

V_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}m_{i}\delta_{(x_{i},P_{i})}\>.

We observe that unlike the case of rectifiable
d
–varifolds, the dimension of the geometric object, i.e.
0
for the set of point, can differ from the dimension
d
of the
d
–varifold which is fixed by the choice of the Grassmannian
{\rm G}_{d,n}
.

Varifolds and Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)

For the purpose of the paper, we will also consider Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
, similarly to what is done in
[
Tin23
,
BP23
]
, see also
[
AS97
]
. As already mentioned, the identification between
T\in{\rm G}_{d,n}
and
\Pi_{T}
the rank
d
orthogonal projector onto
T
provides a natural embedding
i:{\rm G}_{d,n}\hookrightarrow{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
,
T\mapsto\Pi_{T}
that induces the following embedding of
d
–varifolds into Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
:

\mathcal{I}:V\in\{d\text{--varifold in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\}\mapsto W=({\rm id},i)_{\#}V\>,

or equivalently, by definition of pushforward measure, for
f\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n))
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)}f\>dW=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}}f(x,i(T))\>dV(x,T)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}}f(x,\Pi_{T})\>dV(x,T)\>.

In the particular case of the
d
–varifold
V_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{T_{x}S}
associated with the rectifiable set
S
(see Definition
2.15
), we obtain the Radon measure

(6)

\mathcal{I}(V_{S})=W_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{\Pi_{T_{x}S}}\>.

Recalling the notation
{{\rm P}_{d,n}}=\{\text{rank }d\text{--orthogonal projectors}\}\subset{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
, we observe that for a
d
–varifold
V
,
W=\mathcal{I}(V)
is supported in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{{\rm P}_{d,n}}
.
We recall that
i:{\rm G}_{d,n}\rightarrow{{\rm P}_{d,n}}
is a bijective isometry, we can denote by
s_{0}:{{\rm P}_{d,n}}\rightarrow{\rm G}_{d,n}
its inverse and in particular,
i
and
s_{0}
are
1
–Lipschitz maps. Then,
\mathcal{I}
is bijective from
\{d\text{--varifolds in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
onto
\{\text{Radon measures in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)\text{ with support in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{{\rm P}_{d,n}}\}
, with inverse
\mathcal{I}^{-1}:W\mapsto V=\mathds{1}_{{{\rm P}_{d,n}}}\left(({\rm id},s_{0})_{\#}W\right)
i.e., for
g\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}}g\>dV=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}^{1}(n)}g(x,s_{0}(A))\mathds{1}_{{{\rm P}_{d,n}}}(A)\>dW(x,A)\>.

As we detail in the proof of Proposition
2.17
, it is not difficult to check that
\mathcal{I}
is
1
–Lipschitz using that
i
is, whereas it is more subtle to check
that its inverse
\mathcal{I}^{-1}
is Lipschitz since
s_{0}
is only defined on
{{\rm P}_{d,n}}
. More precisely, we have the following bi–Lipschitz correspondence:

Proposition 2.17
.

The map
\mathcal{I}:V\mapsto({\rm id},i)_{\#}V
induces a bi–Lipschitz bijection from
\{d\text{--varifolds in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
onto
\{\text{Radon measures in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)\text{ with support in }\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{{\rm P}_{d,n}}\}
endowed with the bounded Lipschitz distance
\beta
. More precisely, there exists
L\geq 1
depending on
n
such that for any
d
–varifolds
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\beta(\mathcal{I}(V_{1}),\mathcal{I}(V_{2}))\leq\beta(V_{1},V_{2})\leq L\beta(\mathcal{I}(V_{1}),\mathcal{I}(V_{2}))\>.

Proof.

Given two
d
–varifolds
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
and using the notations
W_{1}=\mathcal{I}(V_{1})
and
W_{2}=\mathcal{I}(V_{2})
, it is not difficult to check that

(7)

\beta(W_{1},W_{2})\leq\beta(V_{1},V_{2})\>.

Indeed, given
f\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n))
a
1
–Lipschitz function such that
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, we have that
g=f\circ({\rm id},i)\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n})
is
1
–Lipschitz and satisfies
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq 1
as well whence

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)}f\>(dW_{1}-dW_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}}g\>(dV_{1}-dV_{2})\leq\beta(V_{1},V_{2})\>,

and one can take the supremum with respect to such maps
f
to infer (
7
).

The converse inequality holds true as well, up to some dimensional constant but requires to extend the above map
s_{0}
in a neighbourhood of
{{\rm P}_{d,n}}
while controlling the Lipschitz constant of the extension. We introduce the neighbourhood
\mathcal{V}=\left\{A\in{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)\>:\>{\rm dist}(A,{\rm P}_{d,n})\leq\frac{1}{3}\right\}
of
{{\rm P}_{d,n}}
and we first define
\tilde{s}:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow{{\rm P}_{d,n}}
such that
\tilde{s}_{|{{\rm P}_{d,n}}}={\rm id}_{{\rm P}_{d,n}}
as follows. Let
A\in\mathcal{V}
so that there exists
P\in{\rm P}_{d,n}
satisfying
\|A-P\|\leq\frac{1}{3}
. We denote by
\lambda_{1}(A)\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n}(A)
the ordered eigenvalues of
A
.
Applying Weyl inequality for symmetric matrices, that is for all
k=1\ldots n
,
|\lambda_{k}(A)-\lambda_{k}(P)|\leq\|A-P\|
, we obtain:

\lambda_{d+1}(A)\leq\underbrace{\lambda_{d+1}(P)}_{=0}+\underbrace{\|A-P\|}_{\leq\frac{1}{3}}\leq\frac{1}{3}\quad\text{and}\quad\underbrace{\lambda_{d}(P)}_{=1}\leq\lambda_{d}(A)+\underbrace{\|A-P\|}_{\leq\frac{1}{3}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lambda_{d}(A)\geq\frac{2}{3}>\frac{1}{3}\geq\lambda_{d+1}(A)\>.

We then define
\tilde{s}(A)\in{\rm P}_{d,n}
to be the orthogonal projection matrix onto the direct sum of eigenspaces of
A
from
\lambda_{1}(A)
up to
\lambda_{d}(A)
.
In other words,
\tilde{s}(A)
is obtained by replacing the eigenvalues of
A
with
1
for
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d}
and
0
for the others, while not changing the eigenspaces.
The Lipschitz continuity of
\tilde{s}
in
\mathcal{V}
is a direct consequence of Davis–Kahan Theorem (
[
DK70
]
, see also Theorem 2 in
[
YWS14
]
): let
A,B\in\mathcal{V}
, by Davis–Kahan Theorem,

\|\tilde{s}(A)-\tilde{s}(B)\|_{F}\leq 2\sqrt{2}\frac{\|A-B\|_{F}}{|\lambda_{d+1}(A)-\lambda_{d}(A)|}\leq\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{2}\|A-B\|_{F}\>,

where
\|\cdot\|_{F}
is the Frobenius norm that is equivalent to the operator norm
\|\cdot\|
then implying the Lipschitz continuity of
\tilde{s}
in
\mathcal{V}
with a Lipschitz constant
\tilde{L}\geq 1
only depending on
n
. Then
s=s_{0}\circ\tilde{s}
is
\tilde{L}
–Lipschitz as well and extends
s_{0}
to
\mathcal{V}
.
Given
g\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n})
, we can then write

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm G}_{d,n}}g\>(dV_{1}-dV_{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)}g(x,s(A))\xi_{\mathcal{V}}(A)\>(dW_{1}-dW_{2})\>.

where
\xi_{\mathcal{V}}(A)=1-4\>{\rm dist}(A,{\rm P}_{d,n})
if
{\rm dist}(A,{\rm P}_{d,n})\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
0
otherwise so that
\xi_{\mathcal{V}}
is
4
–Lipschitz continuous and compactly supported in
\left\{A\in{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)\>:\>{\rm dist}(A,{\rm P}_{d,n})\leq\frac{1}{4}\right\}
. Therefore,
f=(g\circ({\rm id},s))\xi_{\mathcal{V}}
if
\tilde{L}+4
–Lipschitz and satisfies
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. We can eventually take the supremum with respect to such maps
g
to infer that for some dimensional constant
L\geq 1
,

(8)

\beta(V_{1},V_{2})\leq L\beta(W_{1},W_{2})\>.

Note that we showed that we could express
\mathcal{I}^{-1}
as
\mathcal{I}^{-1}:W\mapsto V=\xi_{\mathcal{V}}\left(({\rm id},s)_{\#}W\right)
.
∎

Building upon such an explicit bi–Lipschitz correspondence
\mathcal{I}
between
d
–varifolds and Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
with support in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm P}_{d,n}
, we estimate
W_{S}
hereafter, rather than
V_{S}
. We will also abusively call
d
–varifolds such Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
with support in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{{\rm P}_{d,n}}
.

3.
Inference framework

Consistently with what we explained in the introduction, we fix the following setting from now on and all along the paper: we are given a
d
–dimensional closed set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{d}(S)<+\infty
and we assume that we have access to samples of
S
, but possibly not uniformly distributed in
S
. More formally, for
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
are
\mathbb{R}^{n}
–valued independent random variables identically distributed according to a law
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
. In this setting, “uniformly distributed in
S
” would mean that the density
\theta
is constant. We consider hereafter more general Borel densities
\theta:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
, however, a crucial requirement is that
\theta\geq\theta_{min}>0
is lower bounded. Loosely speaking, we exclude the case where the sampling process would produce holes in some parts of
S
. We also assume that
\theta\leq\theta_{max}<+\infty
is upper bounded. We mention that such uniform assumptions could be relaxed regarding the pointwise results in Section
3.2
(see Remark
3.7
) while the “non pointwise” convergence results (involving the Bounded Lipschitz distance) strongly rely on such uniform bounds.
An important point is that the following assumptions

(H_{1})
and
(H_{2})
would automatically imply that
S
is compact even though we do not require it (see Remark
3.1
) and we directly assume it then.
For the sake of clarity, we will thereafter refer to the following hypotheses:

(H_{1})
:

The set
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a compact set satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{d}(S)<+\infty
and
\exists\widetilde{C_{0}}\geq 1
such that
\forall x\in S
,
\forall 0<r\leq{\rm diam}(S)
,

{\widetilde{C_{0}}}^{-1}r^{d}\leq\mathcal{H}^{d}(S\cap B(x,r))\leq\widetilde{C_{0}}r^{d}\>,

and the probability measure
\mu
is defined as
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
with
\theta:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}\in{\rm L}^{1}(\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S})
such that
\int_{S}\theta\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}=1
.

(H_{2})
:

We assume that there exist
0<\theta_{min}\leq\theta_{max}<+\infty
such that for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a. e.
x\in S
,

\theta_{min}\leq\theta(x)\leq\theta_{max}\>.

(H_{3})
:

We assume
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
S
is
d
–rectifiable.

We recall that we introduced in Section
1.1
the regularity class

(9)

\mathcal{Q}=\{\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\>:\>S,\>\theta\text{ satisfy \hyperref@@ii[hypH1]{$(H_{1})$}--\hyperref@@ii[hypH2]{$(H_{2})$}--\hyperref@@ii[hypH3]{$(H_{3})$}}\}

that depends on
d,\widetilde{C_{0}},\theta_{min/max}
or equivalently on
d,C_{0},\theta_{min/max}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Note that:

(1)

If
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
and
(H_{2})
then the measure
\nu:=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
is
d
–Ahlfors regular with regularity constant
\widetilde{C_{0}}
,
\supp\nu=S
, the measure
\mu
is
d
–Ahlfors regular with regularity constant
C_{0}=\widetilde{C_{0}}\max(\theta_{min}^{-1},\theta_{max})
and
S
is bounded even if not required.

(2)

If
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
, it would be equivalent to require that
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
is
d
–Ahlfors regular or
(H_{2})
.

(3)

(H_{3})
implies that
\mu
is
d
–rectifiable.

Indeed, if
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
, let us check that
S=\supp\nu
and then the Ahlfors regularity of
\nu
follows from
(H_{1})
. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus S
, which is an open set, then for
r>0
small enough
S\cap B(x,r)=\emptyset
hence
x\notin\supp\nu
and thus
\supp\nu\subset S
. Conversely, if
x\in S
, then for all
r>0
,
\nu(B(x,r))\geq\widetilde{C_{0}}^{-1}r^{d}>0
hence
x\in\supp\nu
and thus
S\subset\supp\nu
.
The Ahlfors regularity of
\mu
is then straightforward thanks to
(H_{2})
:
let
x\in S
and
0<r\leq{\rm diam}(S)
, then

\widetilde{C_{0}}^{-1}\theta_{min}r^{d}\leq\theta_{min}\nu(B(x,r))\leq\mu(B(x,r))=\int_{B(x,r)}\theta\>d\nu\leq\theta_{max}\nu(B(x,r))\leq\theta_{max}\widetilde{C_{0}}r^{d}

and
S=\supp\mu
(as for
\nu
) so that
\mu
is
d
–Ahlfors regular with regularity constant
C_{0}=\widetilde{C_{0}}\max(\theta_{min}^{-1},\theta_{max})
. As
\mu
is a finite
d
–Ahlfors measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
S=\supp\mu
is automatically bounded thanks to Remark
2.6
.
We thus checked
(i)
.
As for
(ii)
, if
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
and
\mu
is
d
–Ahlfors regular with regularity constant
C_{0}
, then
\theta
satisfy
(H_{2})
by differentiation of Radon measures: for a. e.
x\in S
,

\theta(x)=\lim_{r\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mu(B(x,r))}{\nu(B(x,r))}\quad\text{and}\quad\forall r>0,\,(C_{0}\widetilde{C_{0}})^{-1}\leq\frac{\mu(B(x,r))}{\nu(B(x,r))}\leq C_{0}\widetilde{C_{0}}\>.

In Sections
6
and
7
, further assumptions (

H
4

) to (

H
7

) will be added to this minimal setting leading to the regularity class
\mathcal{P}
(
100
).

3.1.
Some preliminary facts concerning the empirical measure

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathds{P})
be a probability triplet. We fix hereafter some notations and collect some preliminary computations that will be useful in the sequel (more particularly in the proof of Proposition
4.3
).

Definition 3.2

(Empirical measure)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
be
N
independent
\mathbb{R}^{n}
–valued random variables with the same law
\mu
, the associated empirical measure
\mu_{N}
is defined as:

\mu_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{i}}\>.

We recall the following basic properties of the empirical measure. Given
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and an i.i.d. sample
(X_{1},\dots,X_{N})
with common distribution
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have for any Borel sets
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(10)

\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(B)\right]=\mu(B)\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm Var}\left(\mu_{N}(B)\right)=\frac{\mu(B)-\mu(B)^{2}}{N}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(B)^{2}\right]\leq\mu(B)^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\mu(B)\>.

We will also need the following similar though slightly more technical estimates (in the proof of Lemma
4.1
):

Lemma 3.3
.

For any Borel set
A,B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\leq C\min(\mu(A),\mu(B))
(with
C\geq 1
), we have

(11)

\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(A)\mu_{N}(B)\right]\leq 2C\mu(A)\mu(B)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(A)^{2}\mu_{N}(B)^{2}\right]\leq 15C^{3}\mu(A)^{2}\mu(B)^{2}\>.

Assume in addition that
\mu
is
d
–Ahlfors regular with constant
C_{0}\geq 1
, then, for any Borel set
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and for all
0<N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq R,\widetilde{R}
, we have

(12)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{T}\mu_{N}(B(x,R))\>d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x)\right]\leq 3C_{0}R^{d}\mu(T)\>,

(13)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{T}\mu_{N}(B(x,R))\mu_{N}(B(x,\widetilde{R}))\>d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x)\right]\leq 7C_{0}^{2}R^{d}\widetilde{R}^{d}\mu(T)\>.

Proof.

Let us start with the proof of (
11
).
First note that due to the symmetry of the estimates, we can assume that
\mu(A)\leq\mu(B)
without loss of generality.
Then,

\mu_{N}(B)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{i}}(B)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(A)\mu_{N}(B)\right]=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{X_{i}}(A)\delta_{X_{j}}(B)\right]\>.

If
i=j
, then
\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{X_{i}}(A)\delta_{X_{j}}(B)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{X_{i}}(A\cap B)\right]=\mathds{P}(X_{i}\in A\cap B)=\mu(A\cap B)
and otherwise
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
are independent and thus
\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{X_{i}}(A)\delta_{X_{j}}(B)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{X_{i}}(A)\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{X_{j}}(B)\right]=\mu(A)\mu(B)

so that

\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(A)\mu_{N}(B)\right]\leq\mu(A)\mu(B)+\underbrace{\frac{N}{N^{2}}}_{\leq C\mu(A)}\underbrace{\mu(A\cap B)}_{\leq\mu(B)}\leq(1+C)\mu(A)\mu(B)\>.

We are left with the second estimate in (
11
) and we perform similar computations:

\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(A)^{2}\mu_{N}(B)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{N^{4}}\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{X_{i}}(A)\delta_{X_{j}}(A)\delta_{X_{k}}(B)\delta_{X_{l}}(B)\right]\>.

We can then use independence when the four indices are all different and enumerate the other cases, which leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(A)^{2}\mu_{N}(B)^{2}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\mu(A)^{2}\mu(B)^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\mu(A)\mu(B)\left(\mu(A)+\mu(B)+4\mu(A\cap B)\right)+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\left(\mu(A)\mu(B)+2\mu(A\cap B)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{N^{2}}\mu(A\cap B)\left(\mu(A)+\mu(B)\right)+\frac{1}{N^{3}}\mu(A\cap B)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mu(A)^{2}\mu(B)^{2}+\frac{6}{N}\mu(A)\mu(B)^{2}+\frac{7}{N^{2}}\mu(B)^{2}+\frac{1}{N^{3}}\mu(B)\>,

where we used
\mu(A\cap B)\leq\mu(A)\leq\mu(B)
and we conclude the proof of (
11
) with
\frac{1}{N}\leq C\mu(A)
.

We proceed with the proof of (
12
). By linearity and Ahlfors regularity, we have

(14)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{T}\mu_{N}(B(x,R))d\mu(x)\right]=\int_{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(B(x,R))\right]d\mu(x)=\int_{T}\mu(B(x,R))d\mu(x)\leq C_{0}R^{d}\mu(T)\>.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{T}\mu_{N}(B(x,R))d\mu_{N}(x)\right]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(B(X_{i},R))\>\delta_{X_{i}}(T)\right]=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]

and for
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[u(X_{i},X_{j})]\quad\text{with}\quad u(x,y)=\mathds{1}_{\{|x-y|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{x\in T\}}

\displaystyle=\int_{(x,y)\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{2}}u(x,y)\>d\mu(x)d\mu(y)\quad\text{since }(X_{i},X_{j})\sim\mu\otimes\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in T}\int_{\{y\>:\>|x-y|<R\}}\>d\mu(y)d\mu(x)

(15)

\displaystyle=\int_{T}\mu\left(B(x,R)\right)\>d\mu(x)\>,

whereas for
i=j
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]=E\left[\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]=\mu(T)\>.

Therefore, using
N^{-1}\leq R^{d}
,

(16)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{T}\mu_{N}(B(x,R))d\mu_{N}(x)\right]\leq\int_{T}\mu\left(B(x,R)\right)\>d\mu(x)+\frac{1}{N}\mu(T)\leq(C_{0}+1)R^{d}\mu(T)\leq 2C_{0}R^{d}\mu(T)\>,

and we infer (
12
) from (
14
) and (
16
).

We are left with the proof of (
13
) which is similar. We first note that by symmetry of the statement, we can assume for instance
R\leq\widetilde{R}
. Then by assumption
\frac{1}{N}\leq R^{d}
and by Ahlfors regularity,

\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(B(x,R))\mu_{N}(B(x,\widetilde{R}))\right]\leq\mu(B(x,R))\mu(B(x,\widetilde{R}))+\frac{1}{N}\mu(B(x,R))\leq 2C_{0}^{2}R^{d}\widetilde{R}^{d}\>,

so that

(17)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{T}\mu_{N}(B(x,R))\mu_{N}(B(x,\widetilde{R}))\>d\mu(x)\right]\leq 2C_{0}^{2}R^{d}\widetilde{R}^{d}\mu(T)\>.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{T}\mu_{N}(B(x,R))\mu_{N}(B(x,\widetilde{R}))\>d\mu_{N}(x)\right]=\frac{1}{N^{3}}\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{k}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]\>,

and we review the disjoint possibilities hereafter.

If
i=j=k
, we have
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{k}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]=\mu(T)
.

Otherwise, if
i=k
(or similarly
i=j
, replacing
R
with
\widetilde{R}
), we have thanks to (
15
):

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{k}|<\widetilde{R}\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]=\int_{T}\mu\left(B(x,R)\right)\>d\mu(x)\leq C_{0}R^{d}\mu(T)\>,

whereas if
j=k
, with
R\leq\widetilde{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{k}|<\widetilde{R}\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{T}\mu\left(B(x,R)\right)\>d\mu(x)\leq C_{0}R^{d}\mu(T)\>.

Finally, if
(i,j,k)
are distinct,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{j}|<R\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{i}-X_{k}|<\widetilde{R}\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{X_{i}\in T\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[u(X_{i},X_{j},X_{k})\right]\text{ with }u(x,y,z)=\mathds{1}_{\{|x-y|<R\}}\mathds{1}_{\{|x-z|<\widetilde{R}\}}\>\mathds{1}_{\{x\in T\}}

\displaystyle=\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{3}}u(x,y,z)\>d\mu(x)d\mu(y)d\mu(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{T}\mu\left(B(x,R)\right)\mu\left(B(x,\widetilde{R})\right)\>d\mu(x)\leq C_{0}^{2}R^{d}\widetilde{R}^{d}\mu(T)\>.

Enumerating and combining the different cases, we thus obtain using
\frac{1}{N}\leq R^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{T}\mu_{N}(B(x,R))\mu_{N}(B(x,\widetilde{R}))\>d\mu_{N}(x)\right]

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}^{2}R^{d}\widetilde{R}^{d}\mu(T)+\frac{C_{0}}{N}(2R^{d}+\widetilde{R}^{d})\mu(T)+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\mu(T)

\displaystyle\leq 5C_{0}^{2}R^{d}\widetilde{R}^{d}\mu(T)\>,

which concludes the proof of (
13
) thanks to (
17
).
∎

3.2.
Pointwise estimator of density

As a first step in the estimation of
S
, we recall in this section a usual kernel estimator of the density
\theta
and its pointwise mean convergence rate (see Corollary
3.6
). Note that the weak regularity framework does not affect the convergence rate of the “concentration” part (see Proposition
3.5
and Proposition 3.5 in
[
BH21
]
) whose proof is straightforward.
We fix a family of radial kernels in the usual way: we fix a Lipschitz even function
\eta:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
with support in
(-1,1)
and we additionally assume that
\eta>0
in
\left[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right]
and
we define

(18)

C_{\eta}=d\omega_{d}\int_{r=0}^{1}\eta(r)r^{d-1}\>dr\quad\text{and for }\delta>0,\>x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\eta_{\delta}(x)=\eta\left(\frac{|x|}{\delta}\right)\>.

We introduce the following notations that will be used throughout the next sections: given a Radon measure
\lambda
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\delta>0
,
\displaystyle\Theta_{\delta}(x,\lambda)=\frac{\lambda\ast\eta_{\delta}}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}
and when there is no ambiguity, we will use the following shortened notations in the cases
\lambda=\mu
and
\lambda=\mu_{N}
,

(19)

\theta_{\delta}=\Theta_{\delta}(\cdot,\mu)=\frac{\mu\ast\eta_{\delta}}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\quad\text{and}\quad\theta_{\delta,N}=\Theta_{\delta}(\cdot,\mu_{N})=\frac{\mu_{N}\ast\eta_{\delta}}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\>.

We first check in Proposition
3.4
that we have the convergence of
\theta_{\delta}
to
\theta
a.e. in
S
when
\delta
tends to
0
, only assuming that
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
is rectifiable.
We then recall in Proposition
3.5
) the mean convergence of
\theta_{\delta,N}
to
\theta_{\delta}
for a given
\delta>0
. We conclude in Corollary
3.6
that we can choose a sequence
(\delta_{N})_{N}
tending to
0
such that
\theta_{\delta_{N},N}
is a pointwise convergent estimator of the density
\theta
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
1\leq d\leq n
be an integer and let
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
be a
d
–rectifiable measure.
Let
\theta_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
be as in (
19
).
Then for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a.e.
x\in S
,
\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0_{+}}\theta_{\delta}(x)=\theta(x)
.

Proof.

We use the rectifiability of
\mu
implying that
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a.e. in
S
,
\mu
has an approximate tangent plane
T_{x}S
. Let
x\in S
be such a point and apply Definition
2.12
with
\eta_{1}=\eta(|\cdot|)\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
to obtain

\theta_{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta\left(\frac{|y-x|}{\delta}\right)\>d\mu(y)\xrightarrow[\delta\to 0]{}\theta(x)\frac{1}{C_{\eta}}\int_{T_{x}S}\eta(|y|)\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)=\theta(x)\>,

where the last equality follows from the coarea formula applied on concentric spheres:

\displaystyle\int_{T_{x}S}\eta(|y|)d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)=

\displaystyle\int_{r=0}^{1}\int_{\{y\in T_{x}S\>:\>|y|=r\}}\eta(|y|)d\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(y)dr=\int_{r=0}^{1}\eta(r)\underbrace{\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(T_{x}S\cap\partial B(0,1))}_{=d\omega_{d}}r^{d-1}\>dr

(20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\omega_{d}\int^{1}_{0}\eta(r)r^{d-1}dr=C_{\eta}\>.

∎

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
0<d\leq n
and assume that
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
and
(H_{2})
and let
C_{0}\geq 1
be a regularity constant for
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
(see Remark
3.1
(i)
).
Let
\mu_{N}
be the empirical measure associated with
\mu
and
\theta_{\delta}
,
\theta_{\delta,N}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
be as in (
19
).
Then there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0},\eta)>0
such that for all
x\in S
and for all
\delta>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,

(21)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta}(x)\right|\right]\leq\frac{M}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}\>.

Proof.

We fix
x\in S
and define for
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,

Z_{j}=\frac{1}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\eta\left(\frac{|x-X_{j}|}{\delta}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad Z=\theta_{\delta,N}(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}Z_{j}\>.

Since
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
are i.i.d., we have

\mathbb{E}\left[|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta}(x)|^{2}\right]={\rm Var}(Z)=\frac{{\rm Var}(Z_{1})}{N}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[(Z_{1})^{2}]}{N}\leq\frac{\|\eta\|_{\infty}^{2}}{C_{\eta}^{2}N\delta^{2d}}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{j}-x|<\delta\}}]\leq\frac{C_{0}\|\eta\|_{\infty}^{2}}{C_{\eta}^{2}N\delta^{d}}\>.

We conclude the proof of Proposition
3.5
thanks to Jensen inequality:

\mathbb{E}\left[|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta}(x)|\right]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta}(x)|^{2}\right]}\leq M(d,C_{0},\eta)\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}\>.

∎

Note that we do not need
\eta
to be Lipschitz in the proof of Proposition
3.5
, it will be however necessary in Lemma
4.7
and thus in Theorem
4.9
as well. Combining Proposition
3.4
and Proposition
3.5
yields the mean convergence of the kernel estimator of density
\theta_{\delta,N}
provided that
\delta=\delta_{N}\to 0
is well-chosen, as stated in Corollary
3.6
.

Corollary 3.6
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and assume that
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
,
(H_{2})
and
(H_{3})
.
Let
\mu_{N}
be the empirical measure associated with
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
and
\theta_{\delta}
,
\theta_{\delta,N}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
as in (
19
).
Let
(\delta_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}}
be a positive sequence tending to
0
and such that
\delta_{N}N^{\frac{1}{d}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}+\infty
, then for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a.e.
x\in S
,

(22)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\theta_{\delta_{N},N}(x)-\theta(x)\right|\right]\leq M\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta_{N}^{d}}}+|\theta_{\delta_{N}}(x)-\theta(x)|\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

Proof.

Corollary
3.6
is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.5
and Proposition
3.4
.
∎

As we already explained in the introduction, convergence of
\theta_{\delta_{N},N}
does not hold uniformly for
\mu\in\mathcal{Q}
.
Indeed, in order to be more precise concerning both the choice of
\delta_{N}
and the mean convergence rate, it is important to quantify the convergence rate of
\theta_{\delta}
to
\theta
. However, as
illustrated in Example
6.1
, we need to strengthen the regularity framework to this end, which is done in Section
6
and
7
where a piecewise Hölder regularity class
\mathcal{P}
(
100
) is considered. It will be then possible to establish the mean convergence of the estimator
\theta_{\delta_{N},N}
with a uniform choice of
\delta_{N}\to 0
and a uniform convergence rate in the specified regularity class, see Proposition
7.5
.

Before moving to the estimation of the measure
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
, we comment on the upper bound assumption on
\theta
.

Remark 3.7

(Uniform bounds on the density)
.

As mentioned in the introduction of the section, it would be possible to relax the uniform density bounds (or equivalently the Ahlfors regularity assumptions) regarding the convergence of
\theta_{\delta,N}
since Corollary
3.6
is a pointwise result: given
x\in S
, by definition of
\theta(x)=\lim_{\delta\to 0_{+}}\frac{\mu(B(x,\delta))}{\delta^{d}}
it is possible to assume that for radii
\delta
small enough,
\mu(B(x,\delta))\leq C_{x}\delta^{d}
where the constant
C_{x}>0
is not uniform as was
C_{0}
but now depends on
x
.

4.
Estimation of the
d
–dimensional measure carried by
S

In this section, we analyse the convergence of an estimator
\nu_{\delta,N}
(see (
55
)) of the measure
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
obtained by weighting the empirical measure according to the estimated local density
\theta_{\delta,N}
. Such an estimator is commonly implemented as a simple way of balancing a non-uniform sampling, hence worthing some investigations. Assuming stronger regularity of
\theta
and of the underlying set
S
, more intricate constructions interpolating information would achieve better convergence rates as established in
[
AL19
, Theorem
6
]
for the estimation of
S
in Hausdorff distance, as well as in
[
TY23
, Theorem
1
]
concerning the estimation of
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
. It is however interesting to analyse such a commonly used kernel-based estimator in a low-regularity framework despite a deteriorated convergence rate. Of course, a crucial point would be to establish minimax rates in terms of Bounded Lipschitz distance for estimators of
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
in a low-regularity model, for instance in the regularity class
\mathcal{P}
. Yet, we were not able to obtain such minimax rates up to now
and we intend to carry on our investigations on the question in future work.

In
[
Dud69
]
the author quantifies the mean speed convergence of the empirical measure
\mu_{N}
towards the sampled probability law
\mu
in terms of Bounded Lipschitz distance. More precisely (see Theorem
3.2
in
[
Dud69
]
) under some technical assumption ensuring that
\mu
behaves like a
d
–dimensional measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the author established that

(23)

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta(\mu_{N},\mu)\right]\lesssim\frac{1}{N^{d}}\>.

In order to show the convergence of
\nu_{\delta,N}
towards
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
, we adapt the arguments of
[
Dud69
]
but passing from
1
–Lipschitz and bounded by
1
test functions to random test functions with only local bounds on the random Lipschitz constant (see technical assumption (
36
)).
The proof of Proposition
4.3
then follows the structure of the proof of Theorem
3.2
in
[
Dud69
]
: we localize the Bounded Lipschitz distance (see Definition
2.4
) and we adapt the choice of partition larger scale (denoted by “
\varepsilon_{s}
” in the proof) when localizing in balls. Such a localization is natural since the pointwise value of a kernel-based estimator (e.g.
\theta_{\delta,N}
but also the notions of approximate curvature introduced in
[
BLM17
,
BLM22
]
) at some point
x
depends on the empirical measure
\mu_{N}
restricted to the ball
B(x,\delta)
.
From Proposition
4.3
, we straightforwardly infer the convergence of
\nu_{\delta,N}
towards
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
in Theorem
4.9
and Corollary
4.10
. As explained in the introduction, uniform convergence rates are established in Sections
6
and
7
(see more precisely Theorem
7.4
) assuming stronger regularity (in the piecewise Hölder regularity class
\mathcal{P}
(
100
) as defined in Section
6.1
). We will subsequently use Proposition
4.3
when investigating the estimation of the whole varifold measure in Section
6.4
.

The section is organised as follows, in Section
4.1
we first recall and adapt technical ingredients that are then applied in Section
4.2
in order to establish Proposition
4.3
. Section
4.3
is dedicated to the convergence of
\nu_{\delta,N}
to
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
in the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
(i.e. under assumptions
(H_{1})
-
(H_{2})
-
(H_{3})
) as stated in Theorem
4.9
and Corollary
4.10
.

4.1.
Nested partitions and mean discrepancy over partitions

In this section, we introduce technical ingredients already contained in
[
Dud69
]
, except Lemma
4.1

(ii)
, which is an adaptation that will be used in the proof of Proposition
4.3
.
The following lemma estimates the mean discrepancy between
\mu
and
\mu_{N}
over a given partition.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a Borel set and let
S_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,m
, be disjoint Borel sets with union
T
. Then,

(1)

see Proposition 3.1 in
[
Dud69
]

(24)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}|\mu_{N}(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j})|\right]\leq\left(\frac{m\mu(T)}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\>,

(2)

assume that
\mu
is
d
–Ahlfors regular, then for
0<R\leq\widetilde{R}
such that
R\geq N^{-\frac{1}{d}}
and for
x_{j}\in S
,
j=1,\ldots,m
,

(25)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))\>|\mu_{N}(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j})|\right]\leq 2C_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\frac{m\mu(T)}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}R^{d}

and

(26)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},\widetilde{R}))\>|\mu_{N}(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j})|\right]\leq 4C_{0}^{\frac{7}{2}}\left(\frac{m\mu(T)}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}R^{d}\widetilde{R}^{d}\>.

Proof.

We focus on proving
(ii)
. First note that using (
10
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left|\mu_{N}(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j})\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{m}{\rm Var}(\mu_{N}(S_{j}))=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\mu(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j})^{2}}{N}=\frac{1}{N}\left(\mu(T)-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(S_{j})^{2}\right)

(27)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mu(T)}{N}\>.

Then using the
d
–Ahlfors regularity of
\mu
with
C_{0}\geq 1
, we have

\frac{1}{N}\leq R^{d}\leq C_{0}(C_{0}^{-1}R^{d})\leq C_{0}\mu\left(B(x_{j},R)\right)\quad\text{for any }j=1\ldots m\>,

which allows to apply (
11
) and obtain

(28)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))^{2}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))^{2}\right]\leq 2C_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(B(x_{j},R))^{2}\leq 2C_{0}^{3}mR^{2d}\>,

and similarly

(29)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))^{2}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},\widetilde{R}))^{2}\right]\leq 15C_{0}^{3}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu(B(x_{j},R))^{2}\mu(B(x_{j},\widetilde{R}))^{2}\leq 15C_{0}^{7}mR^{2d}\widetilde{R}^{2d}\>.

Furthermore, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))\>|\mu_{N}(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j})|\leq\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left|\mu_{N}(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j})\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\>

and hence, using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality
\mathbb{E}[X^{\frac{1}{2}}\>Y^{\frac{1}{2}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[X]^{\frac{1}{2}}\>\mathbb{E}[Y]^{\frac{1}{2}}
for non-negative random variables
X,Y
, we get

(30)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))\>|\mu_{N}(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j})|\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mu_{N}(B(x_{j},R))^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\times\mathbb{E}\left[\sum^{m}_{j=1}(\mu_{N}(S_{j})-\mu(S_{j}))^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\>.

From (
27
), (
28
) and (
30
) we infer (
25
). Then, (
26
) similarly follows from (
27
), (
29
) and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

Let us notice that as the partition
(S_{j})_{j=1\dots m}
is refined with smaller pieces, then
m
gets larger and the control in the right hand side of (
24
) increases. Nevertheless, it will be important to work with thin enough partitions in the proof of Proposition
4.3
:
we will use that inside a piece of the partition, Lipschitz functions vary at most like the diameter of the piece. The key idea introduced in
[
Dud69
]
to deal with these opposite requirements is to work with a sequence of thinner and thinner nested partitions rather than working with only one thin partition. We refer to
[
Dud69
]
for the construction of such partitions:

Lemma 4.2

(Nested partitions, see
[
Dud69
]
)
.

Let
0<\varepsilon\leq 1
, let
t\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
3^{-(t+1)}<\varepsilon\leq 3^{-t}
and let
s\in\mathbb{N}
,
s\leq t
. Let
T\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded Borel set. We assume that for each integer
s\leq u\leq t
,
T
is the disjoint union of
m_{u}
Borel sets of diameter at most
\varepsilon_{u}:=3^{-u}
. Then, there exists a family of Borel sets

\left\{A_{j}^{u}\>:\>u=s,\ldots,t\text{ and }j=1,\ldots,m_{u}\right\}

such that for all
u
:

(31)

\displaystyle\left(A_{j}^{u}\right)_{j=1\ldots m_{u}}\text{ is a partition of }T\text{ and for all }j,\,{\rm diam}A_{j}^{u}\leq 3\varepsilon_{u}\text{ and }A_{j}^{u}\neq\emptyset\>,

(32)

\displaystyle\text{if }u>s,\text{ for all }q\in\{1,\ldots,m_{u-1}\}\text{ there exists }I_{q,u}\subset\{1,\ldots,m_{u}\}\text{ such that }A_{q}^{u-1}=\bigsqcup_{j\in I_{q,u}}A_{j}^{u}\>.

We could rephrase (
32
) as follows: pieces in the partition at scale
\varepsilon_{u-1}
are formed by unions of pieces from the partition at scale
\varepsilon_{u}
.

4.2.
A technical mean convergence result for a Bounded Lipschitz type term

In order to obtain the convergence of the estimator
\nu_{\delta,N}
(hereafter defined in (
55
)) towards
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}=\frac{1}{\theta}\mu
in terms of localized Bounded Lipschitz distance, more precisely a uniform control on
\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta,N},\nu_{\delta})\right]
in the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
(
9
), we adapt the proof of convergence of
\mathbb{E}\left[\beta(\mu_{N},\mu)\right]
from
[
Dud69
]
.
We note that Corollary
4.10
is sufficient to obtain the convergence of
\nu_{\delta,N}
(Theorem
4.9
),
however, we will need the more general (and probably less comprehensive) Proposition
4.3
later on to prove the convergence of the varifold estimator in Proposition
5.8
. We hence directly state and prove hereafter Proposition
4.3
.
We more precisely adapt the proof of Theorem 3.2 in
[
Dud69
]
to compute a localized Bounded Lipschitz distance as defined in Definition
2.4
, and also to handle random test functions that are not
1
–Lipschitz but satisfy a Lipschitz–type property of the form (
36
), where we use the notation

(33)

\Delta_{t,N}(x,y)=\frac{1}{t^{d}}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,t)\cup B(y,t)\right),\quad\text{for }t>0\text{ and }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\>.

Note that since
\mu_{N}
is a probability measure
\Delta_{t,N}(x,y)\leq t^{-d}
and (
36
) in particular gives a Lipschitz estimate for
g
and
x
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

(34)
