\left|g(x)-g(y)\right|\leq\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta^{d+1}}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r^{d+1}}+\frac{\kappa_{3}}{r^{d}\delta^{d+1}}+\kappa_{0}\right)|x-y|\>.

For the sake of clarity, let us temporarily assume that
0<\delta=r\leq 1
.
Hence directly applying Theorem 3.2 in
[
Dud69
]
with the “deterministic” estimate (
34
) above is possible and we would obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta(\nu_{\delta,N},\nu_{\delta})\right]\lesssim\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta^{2d+1}}\>.

However, applying the following Proposition
4.3
–Corollary
4.10
instead, we obtain in Theorem
4.9
(i)
the significantly improved rate (by a factor
\delta^{2d}
):

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta(\nu_{\delta,N},\nu_{\delta})\right]\lesssim\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta}\>.

The key observation is that we have the following much better Lipschitz estimate for
g
(for small
\delta,r
) after taking the mean value in (
36
) and restricted to
x,y\in\supp\mu
, indeed, using the
d
–Ahlfors regularity:

(35)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|g(x)-g(y)\right|\right]\leq\left(\frac{C_{0}\kappa_{1}+C_{0}^{2}\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{C_{0}\kappa_{2}}{r}+\kappa_{0}\right)|x-y|\>.

Unfortunately, such mean Lipschitz estimate (
35
) does not allow to apply straightforwardly
[
Dud69
]
, though adapting the strategy, we were able to obtain the following result:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and assume that
\mu
is
d
–Ahlfors regular for some real number
0<d\leq n
, with regularity constant
C_{0}\geq 1
(see Definition
2.5
).
For
\delta,r,\kappa,\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in]0,+\infty[
, where
\kappa_{0}
is allowed to depend on
\delta,r
,
1
1

1

For instance
\kappa_{0}=\frac{M}{\delta}+\frac{M}{r}
is used in the proof of Theorem
6.15
.
we denote by
\mathfrak{X}
the set of random Lipschitz functions satisfying
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa\kappa_{0}
and for all
x
,
y\in\supp\mu
,

(36)

\left|g(x)-g(y)\right|\leq\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)+\frac{\kappa_{3}}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)+\kappa_{0}\right)|x-y|\>.

Then,

(1)

case
d>2
and
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
arbitrary bounded open set: there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0})>0
such that for any
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
bounded open set, for all
\delta>0
,
r>0
and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
large enough so that
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\min(\delta,r)
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\left\{\left|\int_{B}g\>d\mu_{N}-\int_{B}g\>d\mu\right|\>:\>g\in\mathfrak{X}\right\}\right]\leq M\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\mu(B^{\gamma_{N}})\\
\quad\text{with }\gamma_{N}=N^{-\frac{d-2}{d^{2}}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

(2)

case
B
open ball of radius
R_{B}>0
and
\kappa\leq R_{B}
: there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0})>0
such that for any open ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
R_{B}<1
, for all
\delta>0
,
r>0
and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
large enough so that
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\min(R_{B},r,\delta)
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\left\{\left|\int_{B}g\>d\mu_{N}-\int_{B}g\>d\mu\right|:g\in\mathfrak{X}\right\}\right]\leq M\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\mu(B)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right.\>.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that assumption (
36
) naturally arises from the fact that we consider kernel–based estimator, for instance in Section
4.3

\eta
is a compactly supported kernel with dilations
\eta_{\delta}=\eta\left(\frac{\cdot}{\delta}\right)
, and thus convolution of the form
\delta^{-d}\mu_{N}\ast\eta_{\delta}
involves (a rough) Lipschitz estimates of the form
\delta^{-1}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)
. Nonetheless, we emphasize that in the piecewise Hölder regularity class
\mathcal{P}
, a finer Lipschitz estimate can be used (see (
145
) in Lemma
7.3
) hence leading to a finer convergence rate in Theorem
7.4
and Proposition
7.5
.

Before proving Proposition
4.3
, we draw an easy consequence (Corollary
4.5
below) that we will directly use in the proof of Theorem
4.9
while in Sections
5
and
6
we will need Proposition
4.3
itself.

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and assume that
\mu
is
d
–Ahlfors regular for some real number
0<d\leq n
, with regularity constant
C_{0}\geq 1
(see Definition
2.5
).
Assume that
h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a random Lipschitz function satisfying: there exist
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}\in[0,+\infty[
such that
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa_{0}
and for all
x
,
y\in\supp\mu
and
\delta
,
r\in]0,+\infty[
,

(37)

\left|h(x)-h(y)\right|\leq\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)|x-y|\>.

Then,

(1)

case
d>2
and
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
arbitrary bounded open set: there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0})>0
such that for any
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
bounded open set and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
\delta>0
satisfying
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\delta
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(h\>\mu_{N},h\>\mu)\right]\leq M\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta}\right)N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\mu(B^{\gamma_{N}})\quad\text{with }\gamma_{N}=N^{-\frac{d-2}{d^{2}}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

and in particular for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,

(38)

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\mu_{N},\mu)\right]\leq MN^{-\frac{1}{d}}\mu(B^{\gamma_{N}})\quad\text{with }\gamma_{N}=N^{-\frac{d-2}{d^{2}}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

(2)

case
B
open ball: there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0})>0
such that for any open ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
0<R_{B}<1
and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
\delta>0
satisfying
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\min(R_{B},\delta)
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(h\>\mu_{N},h\>\mu)\right]\leq M\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}R_{B}}{\delta}\right)\mu(B)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right..

and in particular for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
satisfying
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq R_{B}
,

(39)

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\mu_{N},\mu)\right]\leq M\mu(B)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right..

We recall that
B^{\gamma_{N}}
is the
\gamma_{N}
–enlargement of
B
(see (
2
)).
If we compare the result of Corollary
4.5
: (
38
) and (
39
) with (
23
) obtained in
[
Dud69
]
, we observe that the control is renormalized by the
\mu
–mass of the set
B
relatively to which the distance between
\mu
and
\mu_{N}
is estimated. In (
23
),
B
contains
\supp\mu
and thus has maximal mass
1
.

Proof of Corollary
4.5
.

First of all, (
38
) and (
39
) are direct consequences of the general statement with
h=1
that is,
\kappa_{0}=1
and
\kappa_{1}=0
(see (
37
)).
Then, as already mentioned, Corollary
4.5
is a straightforward consequence of Proposition
4.3
. Indeed, let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open bounded set and
f\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R})
be a
1
–Lipschitz function such that
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\supp f\subset B
. Actually note that
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa
with
\kappa=\min(1,{\rm diam}(B)/2)
and in particular
\kappa\leq R_{B}
in the case
(ii)
where
B
is a ball of radius
R_{B}>0
. Let
h:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a random Lipschitz function satisfying (
37
) then,
g=fh
satisfies
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa\kappa_{0}
and the following Lipschitz estimate type: for all
x
,
y\in\supp\mu
,

\displaystyle\left|g(x)-g(y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq|f(x)||h(x)-h(y)|+|h(y)||f(x)-f(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\kappa\kappa_{1}}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)+\kappa_{0}\right)|x-y|\>,

that is exactly (
36
) with
\widetilde{\kappa_{1}}=\kappa\kappa_{1}
,
\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{3}=0
,

\kappa_{0}
and
\kappa
. Consequently,

\beta_{B}(h\>\mu_{N},h\>\mu)\leq\sup\left\{\left|\int_{B}g\>d\mu_{N}-\int_{B}g\>d\mu\right|\>:\>g\in\mathfrak{X}\right\}

and we can apply Proposition
4.3
to conclude the proof of Corollary
4.5
.
∎

We are left with the proof of Proposition
4.3
:

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open bounded set. We fix
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
\delta>0
and
r>0
satisfying
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\min(r,\delta)
. We use the notations
\varepsilon=N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\in(0,1]
,
\varepsilon_{u}:=3^{-u}
for
u\in\mathbb{N}
, and
T:=B\cap\supp\mu
.

We define integers
0\leq s\leq t
such that

3^{-(t+1)}<\varepsilon\leq 3^{-t}=\varepsilon_{t}\quad\text{and}\quad 3^{-(s+1)}<\varepsilon^{\alpha}\leq 3^{-s}=\varepsilon_{s}\>,

where
0<\alpha\leq 1
is defined hereafter in Step
4
depending on the case
(i)
or
(ii)
.
Thanks to Proposition
2.8
, we know that
T
can be partitioned with
m_{u}
pieces of diameter
\leq\varepsilon_{u}
and

(40)

m_{u}\leq 4^{d}C_{0}\varepsilon_{u}^{-d}\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{u}}{4}}\right)\>.

We can apply Lemma
4.2
to such partitions and define nested partitions
\left\{A_{j}^{u}\>:\>u=s,\ldots,t,\,j=1,\ldots,m_{u}\right\}
satisfying (
31
) and (
32
).
For each
u=s,\dots,t
and
j=1,\dots,m_{u}
we choose
x^{u}_{j}\in A_{j}^{u}
and
we introduce

M_{u}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m_{u}}|\mu_{N}(A^{u}_{j})-\mu(A^{u}_{j})|\quad\text{and}\quad I_{u}:=\left|\sum_{j=1}^{m_{u}}g(x_{j}^{u})(\mu_{N}(A^{u}_{j})-\mu(A^{u}_{j}))\right|\quad\text{for }g\in\mathfrak{X}\>.

Step
1
:
we can prove the following control:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{B}g\>\left(d\mu_{N}-d\mu\right)\right|\leq I_{t}+9\kappa_{0}\varepsilon\left(\mu_{N}(B)+\mu(B)\right)

\displaystyle+9\varepsilon\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta^{d+1}}\int_{T}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)\>d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x)

\displaystyle+9\varepsilon\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r^{d+1}}\int_{T}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10r)\right)\>d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x)

(41)

\displaystyle+9\varepsilon\frac{\kappa_{3}}{\delta^{d+1}r^{d}}\int_{T}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10r)\right)\>d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x).

Indeed, we remind that
(A_{j}^{t})_{j=1}^{m_{t}}
is a partition of
T=B\cap\supp\mu
and for all
j
,
{\rm diam}A_{j}^{t}\leq 3\varepsilon_{t}\leq 9\varepsilon=9N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq 9\delta
, therefore
B(x,\delta)\cup B(x_{j}^{t},\delta)\subset B(x,10\delta)
so that
\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,x_{j}^{t})\leq\delta^{-d}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)
and similarly

\Delta_{r,N}(x,x_{j}^{t})\leq r^{-d}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10r)\right)
.
Using in addition (
31
) and (
36
) we obtain

\displaystyle\Bigg|\int_{B}g\>\left(d\mu_{N}-d\mu\right)\Bigg|=\left|\sum^{m_{t}}_{j=1}\int_{A_{j}^{t}}g(x)\>d(\mu_{N}-\mu)(x)\right|\leq I_{t}+\left|\sum^{m_{t}}_{j=1}\int_{A_{j}^{t}}\left(g(x)-g(x_{j}^{t})\right)\>d(\mu_{N}-\mu)(x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq I_{t}+\sum^{m_{t}}_{j=1}\int\limits_{A_{j}^{t}}\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta^{d+1}}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r^{d+1}}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10r)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\;\;\,+\frac{\kappa_{3}}{\delta^{d+1}r^{d}}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10r)\right)\right)(3\varepsilon_{t})d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x)

\displaystyle\leq I_{t}+9\kappa_{0}\varepsilon\left(\mu_{N}(B)+\mu(B)\right)

\displaystyle+9\varepsilon\int_{T}\left[\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta^{d+1}}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r^{d+1}}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10r)\right)+\frac{\kappa_{3}}{\delta^{d+1}r^{d}}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10r)\right)\right]\>d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x)\>.

Step
2
:
noting that the dependence on
g
only lies in
I_{t}
in the r.h.s. of (
41
), let us check that

(42)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\left\{\left|\int_{B}g\>d\mu_{N}-\int_{B}g\>d\mu\right|\>:\>g\in\mathfrak{X}\right\}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\left\{I_{t}\>:\>g\in\mathfrak{X}\right\}\right]+M\varepsilon\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\mu(B)\>.

Indeed, taking the mean value in the other terms (different from
I_{t}
) of (
41
), we first have
\mathbb{E}\left[\mu_{N}(B)+\mu(B)\right]=2\mu(B)
.
Then, from (
12
) and (
13
), we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\delta^{d}}\int_{T}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)\>d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x)\right]\leq M\mu(T)\quad\text{and similarly with $r$ instead of }\delta\>,

and furthermore

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\delta^{d}r^{d}}\int_{T}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10\delta)\right)\mu_{N}\left(B(x,10r)\right)\>d(\mu_{N}+\mu)(x)\right]\leq M\mu(T)\>,

which yields (
42
).

Step
3
:
we now estimate
I_{t}
:

(43)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\left\{I_{t}\>:\>g\in\mathfrak{X}\right\}\right]\leq\frac{M}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)\left[\kappa\kappa_{0}\varepsilon_{s}^{-\frac{d}{2}}+9\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\sum_{u=s+1}^{t}\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}+1}\right]\>.

First recall that
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa\kappa_{0}
so that we roughly have
I_{s}\leq\kappa\kappa_{0}M_{s}
.
Let now
s-1\leq u\leq t
, using (
32
) and the Lipschitz estimate (
36
) for
g
we obtain the following recurrence relation:

\displaystyle I_{u}

\displaystyle\leq\Bigg|\sum^{m_{u-1}}_{q=1}\sum_{j\in I_{q,u}}\left(g(x_{j}^{u})-g(x_{q}^{u-1})\right)\>\left(\mu_{N}(A_{j}^{u})-\mu(A_{j}^{u})\right)\Bigg|+\Bigg|\sum^{m_{u-1}}_{q=1}g(x_{q}^{u-1})\>\underbrace{\sum_{j\in I_{q,u}}\left(\mu_{N}(A_{j}^{u})-\mu(A_{j}^{u})\right)}_{=\mu_{N}(A_{q}^{u-1})-\mu(A_{q}^{u-1})}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum^{m_{u-1}}_{q=1}\sum_{j\in I_{q,u}}\left(\kappa_{0}+W_{j,q}^{u}(\delta,r)\right)(3\varepsilon_{u-1})\left|\mu_{N}(A_{j}^{u})-\mu(A_{j}^{u})\right|+I_{u-1}

\displaystyle\quad\text{with}\quad W_{j,q}^{u}(\delta,r)=\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}\left(x_{j}^{u},x_{q}^{u-1}\right)+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\Delta_{r,N}\left(x_{j}^{u},x_{q}^{u-1}\right)+\frac{\kappa_{3}}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}\left(x_{j}^{u},x_{q}^{u-1}\right)\Delta_{r,N}\left(x_{j}^{u},x_{q}^{u-1}\right)

(44)

\displaystyle\leq 9\varepsilon_{u}\>\sum^{m_{u}}_{j=1}W_{j,q(j)}^{u}(\delta,r)\left|\mu_{N}(A_{j}^{u})-\mu(A_{j}^{u})\right|+9\kappa_{0}\varepsilon_{u}M_{u}+I_{u-1}\>,

where
q(j)\in\{1,\ldots,m_{u-1}\}
is such that
j\in I_{q,u}
(that is
q(j)
is the index of the set
A_{q(j)}^{u-1}
containing
A_{j}^{u}
).
By induction from
u=t
down to
s
on (
44
) and recalling that
I_{s}\leq\kappa\kappa_{0}M_{s}
, we have the following control:

(45)

I_{t}\leq\kappa\kappa_{0}M_{s}+9\sum_{u=s+1}^{t}\varepsilon_{u}\left[\kappa_{0}M_{u}+\sum^{m_{u}}_{j=1}W_{j,q(j)}^{u}(\delta,r)\left|\mu_{N}(A_{j}^{u})-\mu(A_{j}^{u})\right|\right]\>.

Note that the right hand side of (
45
) is now independent of
g
and we can then proceed with taking the mean value.
We recall that applying Lemma
4.1

(i)
and using the bound (
40
) on
m_{u}
,
\varepsilon_{u}\leq\varepsilon_{s}
and
\displaystyle\mu(T)\leq\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)
we have

(46)

\mathbb{E}\left[M_{u}\right]\leq\left(\frac{m_{u}\mu(T)}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{2^{d}C_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}}\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)\>.

Moreover,
2
2

2

Note that here, we only know that
|x_{j}^{u}-x_{q}^{u-1}|\leq{\rm diam}(A_{q}^{u-1})\leq 3\varepsilon_{u-1}
which is not less than
\delta
or
r
in general. Thus we cannot say that the union of the two balls lie in a larger one of radius proportional to
\delta
as we did when proving Step
1
.

\Delta_{\delta,N}\left(x_{j}^{u},x_{q}^{u-1}\right)\leq\frac{1}{\delta^{d}}\left(\mu_{N}\left(B(x_{j}^{u},\delta)\right)+\mu_{N}\left(B(x_{q}^{u-1},\delta)\right)\right)
and similarly with
r
instead of
\delta
, then by assumption
\min(\delta,r)\geq N^{-\frac{1}{d}}
so that
applying Lemma
4.1

(ii)
with
R,\widetilde{R}=\delta,r
,
x_{j}=x_{q(j)}^{u-1}
,
x_{j}^{u}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\Bigg[\sum^{m_{u}}_{j=1}W_{j,q(j)}^{u}(\delta,r)\left|\mu_{N}(A_{j}^{u})-\mu(A_{j}^{u})\right|\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{m_{u}\mu(T)}{N}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\delta^{d+1}}4C_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}\delta^{d}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r^{d+1}}4C_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}r^{d}+\frac{\kappa_{3}}{\delta^{d+1}r^{d}}60C_{0}^{\frac{7}{2}}\delta^{d}r^{d}\right)

(47)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}}\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)\>.

Coming back to (
45
) using (
47
) and applying (
46
) once more to control
\kappa_{0}M_{u}
and
\kappa\kappa_{0}M_{s}
, we conclude the proof of (
43
) (i.e. Step
2
).

We can now draw an intermediate conclusion in the proof of Proposition
4.3
, before considering more specifically the different cases at hand in
(i)
and
(ii)
. Indeed, thanks to (
42
) and (
43
) we can infer that

Intermediate conclusion:

(48)

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\left\{\left|\int_{B}g\>d\mu_{N}-\int_{B}g\>d\mu\right|\>:\>g\in\mathfrak{X}\right\}\right]\\
\leq M\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\varepsilon\mu(B)+\frac{M}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left[\kappa_{0}\kappa\varepsilon_{s}^{-\frac{d}{2}}+\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\sum_{u=s+1}^{t}\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}+1}\right]\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)\>.

Step
4
:

We are left with estimating
\displaystyle\sum_{u=s+1}^{t}\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}+1}
whose computation depends on the sign of
-\frac{d}{2}+1
.

– case
d>2
:

Note that for
d>2
,
\varepsilon_{u}^{-d/2+1}=(3^{-u})^{-d/2+1}=(3^{d/2-1})^{u}
and
3^{d/2-1}>1
so that

(49)

\sum_{u=s+1}^{t}\varepsilon_{u}^{-d/2+1}\leq\sum_{u=0}^{t}(3^{d/2-1})^{u}\leq\frac{(3^{d/2-1})^{t+1}}{3^{d/2-1}-1}\leq\frac{3^{d/2-1}}{3^{d/2-1}-1}\varepsilon_{t}^{-d/2+1}\leq M\varepsilon^{-d/2+1}\leq MN^{1/2}\varepsilon\>.

In addition, in the case where
B
is an arbitrary bounded open set,
\kappa\leq 1
and by choosing
\alpha=\frac{d-2}{d}
we have

(50)

\kappa\varepsilon_{s}^{-d/2}\leq\varepsilon^{-\alpha d/2}=\varepsilon^{-d/2+1}=N^{1/2}\>\varepsilon\text{ and }\varepsilon_{s}\leq 3\varepsilon^{\alpha}=3\gamma_{N}\>,

then together with (
49
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\left[\kappa_{0}\kappa\varepsilon_{s}^{-\frac{d}{2}}+\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\sum_{u=s+1}^{t}\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}+1}\right]\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)

(51)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\kappa_{0}N^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon+\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)MN^{\frac{1}{2}}\varepsilon\right)\mu\left(B^{\gamma_{N}}\right)\leq M\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\varepsilon\mu\left(B^{\gamma_{N}}\right)\>.

We can then conclude the proof of
(i)
(
d>2
thanks to (
48
) and (
51
).

If now
B
is an open ball of radius
R_{B}<1
and
\kappa\leq R_{B}
. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
be large enough so that
\varepsilon=N^{-1/d}\leq R_{B}<1
and let
0<\gamma\leq 1
such that
R_{B}=\varepsilon^{\gamma}
. By choosing
\alpha=1+\frac{2}{d}(\gamma-1)
, we have
\alpha-\gamma=\frac{d-2}{d}(1-\gamma)
hence
0<\gamma\leq\alpha\leq 1
and

(52)

\kappa\varepsilon_{s}^{-d/2}\leq R_{B}\varepsilon^{-\alpha d/2}=\varepsilon^{-d/2+1}=N^{1/2}\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\varepsilon_{s}\leq 3\varepsilon^{\alpha}\leq 3\varepsilon^{\gamma}=3R_{B}\>.

From (
48
), (
49
) and (
52
) we infer as before that

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup\left\{\left|\int_{B}g\>d\mu_{N}-\int_{B}g\>d\mu\>\right|\>:\>g\in\mathfrak{X}\right\}\right]\leq M(d,C_{0})\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\mu\left(B^{(R_{B})}\right)\>.

Note that
\mu\left(B^{(R_{B})}\right)=\mu(2B)\leq 2^{d}C_{0}^{2}\mu(B)
by Ahlfors regularity which concludes the proof of
(ii)
for
d>2
.

case
d=2
:
in this case,
B
is an open ball of radius
R_{B}\geq\kappa
satisfying
\varepsilon=N^{-1/d}<R_{B}<1
, as
d=2
, we have

N^{-1/2}=\varepsilon
,
\varepsilon_{u}^{-d/2+1}=1
, and by choosing
0<\alpha<1
such that
\varepsilon^{\alpha}=R_{B}
then
\kappa\varepsilon_{s}^{-d/2}\leq R_{B}\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\leq 1
and we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\left[\kappa_{0}\kappa\varepsilon_{s}^{-\frac{d}{2}}+\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\sum_{u=s+1}^{t}\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}+1}\right]\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)

(53)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\kappa_{0}+\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)|t-s|\right)\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)\leq\left(\kappa_{0}+\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\ln N\right)\varepsilon\mu\left(2B\right)

since
\varepsilon_{s}\leq 3\varepsilon^{\alpha}\leq 3R_{B}
and
\displaystyle|t-s|\leq t\leq\frac{|\ln(\varepsilon)|}{\ln 3}\leq\frac{\ln N}{2\ln 3}
.
Using the
2
–Ahlfors regularity of
\mu
together with (
48
) and (
53
) concludes the proof of
(ii)
for
d=2
and eventually the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

– case
d<2
:
with the same computations as for
d=2
and choosing
\varepsilon^{\alpha}=R_{B}
, we still have
\kappa\varepsilon_{s}^{-d/2}\leq R_{B}^{1-\alpha d/2}\leq 1
. Moreover,
\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}+1}=(3^{d/2-1})^{u}
with
3^{d/2-1}<1
so that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\left[\kappa_{0}\kappa\varepsilon_{s}^{-\frac{d}{2}}+\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\sum_{u=s+1}^{t}\varepsilon_{u}^{-\frac{d}{2}+1}\right]\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)

(54)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\kappa_{0}+\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\frac{1}{1-3^{d/2-1}}\right)\mu\left(B^{\frac{\varepsilon_{s}}{4}}\right)\leq MN^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\kappa_{0}+\frac{\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{3}}{\delta}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{r}\right)\mu\left(2B\right)\>.

∎

4.3.
Estimation of the measure carried by S

In Section
3.2
, we have established the pointwise convergence of the estimator
\theta_{\delta_{N},N}
towards
\theta
when
N\rightarrow+\infty
and
\delta_{N}
suitably chosen. In this section, our purpose is to build upon this result to design an estimator of
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
. More precisely, given
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
\delta>0
, we introduce the measure
\nu_{\delta}
as well as the random measure
\nu_{\delta,N}
obtained by attributing weights to the points of the sample
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N})
as follows:

(55)

\nu_{\delta,N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(X_{i}))\delta_{X_{i}}=\left(\Phi\circ\theta_{\delta,N}\right)\mu_{N}\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{\delta}=\left(\Phi\circ\theta_{\delta}\right)\mu\>,

where
\Phi
is a truncation of the inverse function, for
0<\tau\leq 1
and
t>0
,

(56)

\Phi(t)=\frac{\chi_{\tau}(t)}{t}\quad\text{and}\quad\chi_{\tau}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{ if }0<t<\frac{\tau}{2}\\
\frac{2}{\tau}t-1&\text{ if }\frac{\tau}{2}\leq t\leq\tau\\
1&\text{ if }t>\tau\end{array}\right.\>.

Let us comment on these definitions. Observe that
\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}=\frac{1}{\theta}\>\mu
and it would be natural to similarly consider the random measure
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta_{\delta,N}}\>\mu_{N}
, however, while we assume that
\theta
is uniformly lower bounded, it is more delicate concerning
\theta_{\delta,N}
: it will be possible to infer a lower bound for
\mathbb{E}\left[\theta_{\delta,N}\right]
and not directly for
\theta_{\delta,N}
. To circumvent this point, we multiply the inverse function by the cutoff
\chi_{\tau}
.

Remark 4.6
.

Note that (
55
) defines finite measures though not probability measures in general.

We start with some elementary properties of
\Phi
and
\theta_{\delta,N}
that will be subsequently useful.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\Phi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\theta_{\delta,N}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be defined as in (
56
) and (
19
), then

(1)

\Phi
is bounded and Lipschitz:
\displaystyle\|\Phi\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{\tau}
and
\displaystyle{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\leq\frac{4}{\tau^{2}}
,

(2)

\theta_{\delta,N}
is Lipschitz and more precisely, for all
x
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\left|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta,N}(y)\right|\leq\frac{{\rm Lip}(\eta)}{C_{\eta}}\underbrace{\frac{\mu_{N}\left(B(x,\delta)\cup B(y,\delta)\right)}{\delta^{d}}}_{\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)}\frac{|x-y|}{\delta}\>.

Proof.

We first check
(i)
,
\Phi_{\tau}
is continuous and piecewise smooth and

\Phi_{\tau}^{\prime}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{ if }0<t<\frac{\tau}{2}\\
+\frac{1}{t^{2}}&\text{ if }\frac{\tau}{2}<t<\tau\\
-\frac{1}{t^{2}}&\text{ if }t>\tau\end{array}\right.\>.

We deduce that
{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\leq\frac{4}{\tau^{2}}
and
||\Phi(t)||_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{\tau}
.
Concerning
(ii)
, for all
x
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta,N}(y)|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}|\mu_{N}*\eta_{\delta}(x)-\mu_{N}*\eta_{\delta}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\int_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\eta\left(\frac{|x-z|}{\delta}\right)-\eta\left(\frac{|y-z|}{\delta}\right)\right|d\mu_{N}(z)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}{\rm Lip}(\eta)\frac{|x-y|}{\delta}\mu_{N}(B(x,\delta)\cup B(y,\delta))\>.

∎

By triangular inequality, we can write

(57)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta,N},\>\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S})\right]\leq\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta},\>\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S})+\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta,N},\>\nu_{\delta})\right]

and we study the convergence of
\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta},\>\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S})
in Proposition
4.8
, then
\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta,N},\>\nu_{\delta})\right]
in Theorem
4.9
before concluding in Corollary
4.10
. Note that in the three aforementioned statements, we assume that
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{|S}
with
S
,
\theta
satisfying
(H_{1})
,
(H_{2})
and
(H_{3})
and we recall that it is by definition equivalent to
\mu\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and assume that
S
,
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
,
(H_{2})
and
(H_{3})
and let
C_{0}\geq 1
be a regularity constant of
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
(see Remark
3.1
(i)
).
We recall that
\theta_{\delta}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is defined as in (
19
). Then,
there exists
m=m(d,C_{0},\eta)>0
such that

(58)

\forall\delta>0,\,\forall x\in S,\,\theta_{\delta}(x)\geq m\quad\text{and}\quad\theta(x)\geq m\>.

Assume that
0<\tau\leq m
and let
\chi_{\tau}:(0,+\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
be as in (
56
), then,

\forall\delta>0,\,\forall x\in S,\quad\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta}(x)\right)=\frac{1}{\theta_{\delta}(x)}\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi\left(\theta(x)\right)=\frac{1}{\theta(x)}\>,

and for any bounded open set
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta},\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S})\leq|\nu_{\delta}-\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}|(B)\leq\int_{B}\left|\frac{1}{\theta_{\delta}}-\frac{1}{\theta}\right|\>d\mu\xrightarrow[\delta\to 0_{+}]{}0\>.

Proof.

Let
\displaystyle m_{\eta}=\min\left\{\eta(t)\>:\>t\in\left[0,\tfrac{1}{2}\right]\right\}>0
since
\eta
is supposed to be positive and continuous in
\left[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right]
.
Let
x\in S
and
\delta>0
,

\theta_{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\int_{B(x,\delta)\cap S}\eta\left(\frac{|x-y|}{\delta}\right)\theta(y)\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)\geq m_{\eta}\frac{\mu\left(B\left(x,\frac{\delta}{2}\right)\right)}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\geq 2^{-d}m_{\eta}C_{\eta}^{-1}C_{0}^{-1}\>,

and since
\theta\geq\theta_{min}>0
, we can infer (
58
).

Let now
0<\tau\leq m
, then for all
x\in S
and
\delta>0
,
\theta_{\delta}(x)\geq\tau
and
\theta(x)\geq\tau
hence
\displaystyle\chi_{\tau}(\theta_{\delta}(x))=\chi_{\tau}(\theta(x))=1
. Furthermore, we already know that
\theta_{\delta}\xrightarrow[\delta\to 0_{+}]{}\theta
a.e. in
S
thanks to Proposition
3.4
and by (
58
)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\theta_{\delta}}-\frac{1}{\theta}\right|\leq\frac{2}{m}
in
S
.
As
\mu(B)<+\infty
, we conclude by dominated convergence that

(59)

\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta},\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S})\leq|\nu_{\delta}-\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}|(B)\leq\int_{B}\left|\frac{1}{\theta_{\delta}}\chi_{\tau}(\theta_{\delta})-\frac{1}{\theta}\right|\>d\mu=\int_{B\cap S}\left|\frac{1}{\theta_{\delta}}-\frac{1}{\theta}\right|\>d\mu\xrightarrow[\delta\to 0_{+}]{}0\>.

∎

Note that we similarly have the uniform upper bound, for
x\in S
:

(60)

\theta_{\delta}(x)=\frac{1}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\int_{B(x,\delta)\cap S}\eta\left(\frac{|x-y|}{\delta}\right)\>d\mu(y)\leq\frac{\|\eta\|_{\infty}}{C_{\eta}}\frac{\mu\left(B\left(x,\delta\right)\right)}{\delta^{d}}\leq\frac{\|\eta\|_{\infty}C_{0}}{C_{\eta}}\leq M(C_{0},\eta)\>.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
0<d\leq n
and assume that
S
,
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
and
(H_{2})
and let
C_{0}\geq 1
be the regularity constant of
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
(see Remark
3.1
(i)
).
Let
\nu_{\delta}
and
\nu_{\delta,N}
be defined as in (
55
). Then,

(1)

case
d>2
and
B
arbitrary bounded open set: there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0},{\rm Lip}(\eta))>0
such that for any
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
bounded open set and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
0<\delta<1
satisfying
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\delta
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta,N},\>\nu_{\delta})\right]\leq\frac{M}{\tau^{2}}\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta}\mu(B^{\gamma_{N}})\quad\text{with }\gamma_{N}=N^{-\frac{d-2}{d^{2}}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

(2)

case
B
open ball: there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0},{\rm Lip}(\eta))>0
such that for any open ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
0<R_{B}<1
and for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
0<\delta<1
satisfying
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\min(R_{B},\delta)
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta,N},\>\nu_{\delta})\right]\leq\frac{M}{\tau^{2}}\frac{R_{B}}{\delta}\mu(B)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right.\>.

Proof.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and
\delta>0
satisfying
\delta\geq N^{-\frac{1}{d}}
.
Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open bounded set and
T=B\cap S
.
Let
f\in{{\rm C}}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R})
be a
1
–Lipschitz function such that
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\supp f\subset B
.

\displaystyle\left|\int_{B}f\>d\nu_{\delta,N}-\int_{B}f\>d\nu_{\delta}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{B}f\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N})\>d\mu_{N}-\int_{B}f\Phi(\theta_{\delta})\>d\mu\right|

(61)

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{B}f\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N})\>\left(d\mu_{N}-d\mu\right)\right|+\|f\|_{\infty}\int_{B}\left|\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N})-\Phi(\theta_{\delta})\right|\>d\mu\>.

We first deal with the second term in the right hand side of (
61
). Thanks to Lemma
4.7
and Proposition
3.5
, there exists
C=C(d,C_{0},\eta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{B}\left|\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N})-\Phi(\theta_{\delta})\right|\>d\mu\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\tau^{2}}\int_{B}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\theta_{\delta,N}-\theta_{\delta}\right|\right]\>d\mu\leq\frac{M}{\tau^{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}\mu(B)=\frac{M}{\tau^{2}}\left(\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\mu(B)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M}{\tau^{2}}\frac{1}{\delta}\mu(B)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right.\>,

where the last inequality follows from the assumption
\displaystyle\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta}\leq 1
implying

\displaystyle\left(\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta}\right)^{\frac{d}{2}}\leq\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta}
for
d\geq 2
, while for
d<2
, we have
\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}=N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{-\frac{d}{2}}\leq N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\delta^{-1}
since
0<\delta<1
.

As for the first term in the right hand side of (
61
), we can apply Corollary
4.5
with
h:=\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N})
. Indeed, thanks to Lemma
4.7
,
\|h\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{\tau}
and for all
x
,
y\in S
,

\displaystyle|h(x)-h(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(x)\right)-\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(y)\right)\right|\leq\frac{4}{\tau^{2}}\left|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta,N}(y)\right|\leq\frac{4}{\tau^{2}}\frac{{\rm Lip}(\eta)}{C_{\eta}}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)\frac{|x-y|}{\delta}\>,

which concludes the proof of Theorem
4.9

applying Corollary
4.5
with
\kappa_{0}=\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tau}
and
\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=\frac{4{\rm Lip}(\eta)}{\tau^{2}C_{\eta}}
, and additionally noting that in the case where
B
is a ball of radius
0<R_{B}<1
one has
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq R_{B}
in (
61
).
∎

Eventually combining Proposition
4.8
, Theorem
4.9
and triangular inequality (
57
)
leads to the Corollary
4.10
. Note that unlike Theorem
4.9
, the convergence obtained in Corollary
4.10
is no longer uniform in the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
for it is not uniform either in Proposition
4.8
.

Corollary 4.10
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
and assume that
S
,
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
,
(H_{2})
and
(H_{3})
and let
C_{0}\geq 1
be a regularity constant of
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
.
Let
\nu_{\delta}
and
\nu_{\delta,N}
be defined as in (
55
).
Let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open ball of radius
0<R_{B}<1
and
let
\nu_{\delta,N}
be defined as in (
55
). Let
(\delta_{N})_{N}\subset(0,1)^{\mathbb{N}^{\ast}}
be a sequence satisfying

\delta_{N}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{\delta_{N}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right.\>.

Then, there exists a constant
m=m(d,C_{0},{\rm Lip}(\eta))\in(0,1)
only depending on
d
,
C_{0}
and
\eta
such that for any
0<\tau\leq m
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta_{N},N},\>\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S})\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

Remark 4.11

(Choice of the parameter
\tau
)
.

It is possible to assume that
\tau
is fixed and chosen so that
\tau\in\left[\frac{m}{2},m\right]
(with the notation of Proposition
4.8
above) and therefore
\tau
only depends on
d
,
C_{0}
,
\eta
and could be absorbed in the generic rate convergence constant
M
in the above statements (Theorem
4.9
– Corollary
4.10
). However, we note that
\tau
does not only appear in such a constant in front of the convergence rate: choosing
\tau
is already required to define the estimator
\nu_{\delta_{N},N}
. If we drop the
\tau
dependency, we implicitly assume that we are able to fulfill the requirement
\frac{m}{2}\leq\tau\leq m
, that relies on
C_{0}
that may not be explicit in general, and we would need to estimate it first. Another possibility is to keep track of the
\tau
dependency and choose
(\tau_{N})_{N}\subset(0,1)^{\mathbb{N}^{\ast}}
tending to
0
to define
\nu_{\delta_{N},N}
and under the assumptions of Corollary
4.10
, we first retrieve

\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta_{N},N},\>\nu_{\delta_{N}})\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\quad\text{as soon as}\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\tau_{N}^{2}\delta_{N}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

Then, for
N\geq N_{0}
large enough, we have
\tau_{N}\leq m
and the conclusion of Proposition
4.8
still holds for
\delta_{N}\to 0
:

\beta_{B}(\nu_{\delta_{N}},\>\nu)\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>,

so that the convergence result stated in Corollary
4.10
is true provided that
\delta_{N}
is adapted to
\tau_{N}
. In the next sections, we do not keep track of constant
\tau
and rather absorb the
\tau
dependency in a generic constant denoted by
M
.
Nonetheless, estimating such a parameter
\tau
remains an important issue as already mentioned at the very end of Section
1.2
.

5.
Tangent space estimation and varifold-type estimation

The next step towards the varifold inference from
\mu_{N}
is to define a convergent estimator for the tangent space. We analyse a classical way of estimating the tangent space at
x
relying on a weighted covariance matrix centered at
x
. We investigate both the case where the covariance matrix is computed directly from
\mu_{N}
(i.e. considering
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}
as defined in (
70
)) or from
\nu_{\delta,N}
, that is after correcting the density (i.e. considering
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta,N})
according to Definition
5.2
).

In Section
5.1
, we introduce the two aforementioned tangent space estimators
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}
and
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta,N})
whose definitions rely on the notion of covariance matrix associated with a given measure Definition
5.2
. On one hand, we emphasize that such a covariance matrix
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\lambda)=(C_{\varphi}r^{d})^{-1}\lambda\ast\psi_{r}
is kernel based, similarly to the way
\Theta_{\delta}(\cdot,\lambda)=(C_{\eta}\delta^{d})^{-1}\eta_{\delta}\ast\lambda
is defined, and consequently some arguments are similar in the proof of Proposition
5.6
for
\Sigma_{r}
(see also the proof of Proposition
3.5
for
\Theta_{\delta}
). On the other hand, the a.e. convergence of the deterministic part
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})
and
\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)
towards
\Pi_{T_{x}S}
is established in Proposition
5.4
. We then combine both Proposition
5.4
and (
73
) to state the mean convergence of the tangent space estimators
\sigma_{r_{N},\delta_{N},N}
and
\Sigma_{r_{N}}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta_{N},N})
in the second part of Proposition
5.6
, though without uniform rates nor uniform choice of
(\delta_{N})_{N}
,
(r_{N})_{N}\to 0
in the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
. Handling such a lack of uniformity issue is then the purpose of Sections
6
and
7
.
In Section
5.2
, we build upon such tangent space estimators to introduce two varifold–type estimators
W_{r,\delta,N}
and
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
(see (
78
)) of
W_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{\Pi_{T_{x}S}}
.
Thanks to the a.e. pointwise convergence obtained for
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})
and
\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)
towards
\Pi_{T_{x}S}
in Proposition
5.6
, it is possible to obtain their
{\rm L}^{1}(\mu)
–convergence that leads to the convergence of the deterministic part
\beta(W_{r,\delta},W_{S})
,
\beta(\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta},W_{S})
in Proposition
5.9
. The mean convergence of
W_{r,\delta,N}
and
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
towards
W_{r,\delta}
and
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}
(see (
79
)) is obtained in Proposition
5.8
by application of Proposition
4.3
. The resulting convergence
\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(W_{r_{N},\delta_{N},N},W_{S})\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0
is finally stated in Corollary
5.11
, with the same lack of uniform rate in the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
as mentioned for the pointwise tangent space and density estimators.

Remark 5.1

(Constant
M
)
.

All along the current section,
M
stands for a generic constant that may vary from one statement to another one, and only depends on
n,d,C_{0},\eta,\varphi
.

5.1.
Pointwise tangent space estimator

We now fix another family of radial kernels: given a Lipschitz even function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{+}
, compactly supported in
(-1,1)
, we define

(65)

C_{\varphi}=\omega_{d}\int_{t=0}^{1}\varphi(t)t^{d+1}\>dt\quad\text{and for }r>0,\>x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\varphi_{r}(x)=\varphi\left(\frac{|x|}{r}\right)\>.

We also associate with
\varphi
the Lipschitz and compactly supported function
\psi=\psi_{\varphi}\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
defined as follows, for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we recall that
z\otimes z
is the matrix of
(i,j)
–coefficient
z_{i}z_{j}
and we set

(66)

\psi(z)=\varphi(|z|)z\otimes z\in{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)\quad\text{and for }r>0,\,\psi_{r}(z)=\psi\left(\frac{z}{r}\right)\>.

Let us check that
\psi
is bounded and Lipschitz, and more precisely

(67)

\supp\psi\subset B(0,1),\quad\|\psi\|_{\infty}\leq\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm Lip}(\psi)\leq\|\varphi\|_{\infty}+{\rm Lip}(\varphi)\>.

We recall that the matrix
z\otimes z
is the orthogonal projector onto the line spanned by
z
if
|z|=1
and consequently, for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\|z\otimes z\|=|z|^{2}
.
The first two assertions are then consequences of the definitions of
\varphi
and
\psi
, and we are left with the Lipschitz property.
Let
z,w,x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
(z\otimes z)x=(x\cdot z)z
and thus by triangular and Cauchy-Schwarz inequalities,
|\left((z\otimes z)-(w\otimes w)\right)x|=|(x\cdot z)z-(x\cdot w)w|\leq\left(|z|+|w|\right)|z-w||x|
so that

\|z\otimes z-w\otimes w\|\leq\left(|z|+|w|\right)|z-w|\>.

If
z,w\in B(0,1)
, we then have

\displaystyle\|\psi(z)-\psi(w)\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\|z\otimes z-w\otimes w\|+\|w\otimes w\||\varphi(|z|)-\varphi(|w|)|

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi\|_{\infty}(|z|+|w|)|z-w|+|w|^{2}{\rm Lip}(\varphi)|z-w|

\displaystyle\leq\left(2\|\varphi\|_{\infty}+{\rm Lip}(\varphi)\right)|z-w|\>.

If now
z\in B(0,1)
and
w\notin B(0,1)
, then
\psi(w)=\varphi(w)=0
and

\|\psi(z)-\psi(w)\|=\|\varphi(z)z\otimes z-\varphi(w)z\otimes z\|\leq|z|^{2}{\rm Lip}(\varphi)|z-w|\>,

and we can conclude the proof of (
67
).

Definition 5.2

(Covariance matrix associated with a measure)
.

Let
0<d\leq n
,
r>0
and let
\lambda
be a Radon measure in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We define for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the
n\times n
matrix

\Sigma_{r}(x,\lambda)=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\varphi\left(\frac{|y-x|}{r}\right)\frac{y-x}{r}\otimes\frac{y-x}{r}\>d\lambda(y)=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\psi_{r}(y-x)\>d\lambda(y)=\frac{\lambda\ast\psi_{r}(x)}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\>.

Note that
\Sigma_{r}(x,\lambda)\in{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
.

As it will be useful hereafter, we point out that if
\lambda
is
d
–Ahlfors regular with regularity constant
C_{0}
, then for all
x\in\supp\lambda
,

(68)

\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\lambda)\|\leq\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\lambda(B(x,r))\leq\frac{C_{0}}{C_{\varphi}}\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\leq M(C_{0},\varphi)\>.

In geometric measure theory, the minimal assumption allowing to define a notion of tangent space is rectifiability. We show in Proposition
5.3
that as soon as we consider a
d
–rectifiable measure,
\Sigma_{r}
converges almost everywhere towards the approximate tangent space.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
be a
d
–rectifiable measure, then for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a. e.
x\in S
,

\forall r>0,\,\Sigma_{r}\left(x,\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|x+T_{x}S}\right)=\Pi_{T_{x}S}\quad\text{and}\quad\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\xrightarrow[r\to 0_{+}]{}\theta(x)\Pi_{T_{x}S}\>,

where we recall that
\Pi_{T_{x}S}
is the matrix of orthogonal projection on the approximate tangent space
T_{x}S
.
In particular, for
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
,
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)\xrightarrow[r\to 0_{+}]{}\Pi_{T_{x}S}
.

Proof.

For
r>0
and
x\in S
(such that
T_{x}S
exists), we have by translation and dilation:

\Sigma_{r}\left(x,\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|x+T_{x}S}\right)=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\cap T_{x}S}\psi\left(\frac{y}{r}\right)\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\cap T_{x}S}\psi(z)\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(z)}_{=:\Sigma(x)}\>.

Moreover, as
\psi
is continuous and compactly supported, by definition of approximate tangent plane (see Definition
2.12
and Proposition
2.13
), for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a. e.
x\in S
,

\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\psi\left(\frac{y-x}{r}\right)\>d\mu(y)\xrightarrow[r\to 0_{+}]{}\theta(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\cap T_{x}S}\psi(z)\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(z)=\theta(x)\Sigma(x)\>,

and it remains to check that
\Sigma(x)=\Pi_{T_{x}S}
.
Let
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
be the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Let
A\in\mathrm{M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
be an orthogonal matrix such that
T_{x}S=A\>\mathrm{span}\>(e_{1},\dots,e_{d})
and we define for all
i=1,\ldots,n
,
\tau_{i}=Ae_{i}
so that
(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{d})
is an orthonormal basis of
T_{x}S
. Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{0\}
, then
|Ay|=|y|
and
(Ay\cdot e_{i})=\sum_{k=1}^{d}a_{ik}y_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}(\tau_{k}\cdot e_{i})y_{k}
, and
thanks to the change of variables
z=Ay
, we obtain noting that
A
is an isometry:

\displaystyle(\Sigma(x))_{ij}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{0\}}\varphi(|Ay|)(Ay\cdot e_{i})(Ay\cdot e_{j})\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)=\sum_{k,l=1}^{d}(\tau_{k}\cdot e_{i})(\tau_{l}\cdot e_{j})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{0\}}\varphi(|Ay|)y_{k}y_{l}\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)

(69)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l=1}^{d}(\tau_{k}\cdot e_{i})(\tau_{l}\cdot e_{j})\int_{r=0}^{\infty}\varphi(r)\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{0\}\cap S(0,r)}y_{k}y_{l}\>d\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(y)\>dr\>.

Note that if
k\neq l
, by symmetry,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{0\}\cap S(0,r)}y_{k}y_{l}d\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(y)=0
, and if
k=l
:

\int_{S(0,r)}y_{k}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(y)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{m=1}^{d}\int_{S(0,r)}y_{m}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(y)=\frac{1}{d}r^{2}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(S(0,r))=r^{d+1}\omega_{d}\>.

Using the above observations in (
69
) we can conclude the proof of Proposition
5.3
:

(\Sigma(x))_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}(\tau_{k}\cdot e_{i})(\tau_{k}\cdot e_{j})\omega_{d}\int_{r=0}^{\infty}\varphi(r)r^{d+1}\>dr=C_{\varphi}\left(\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right)_{ij}\>.

∎

Proposition
5.3
suggests at least two strategies in order to estimate the approximate tangent space at
x\in S
:

(1)

as
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)\xrightarrow[r\to 0_{+}]{}\Pi_{T_{x}S}
, one can consider
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)
,
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})
and
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})
,

(2)

or similarly, as
\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\xrightarrow[r\to 0_{+}]{}\theta(x)\Pi_{T_{x}S}
, one can alternatively consider

(70)

\sigma_{r}(x)=\Phi(\theta(x))\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu),\quad\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)=\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta}(x)\right)\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)=\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(x)\right)\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})\>,

and we recall that for
x\in S
,
\Phi(\theta(x))=\frac{1}{\theta(x)}
and
\Phi(\theta_{\delta}(x))=\frac{1}{\theta_{\delta}(x)}
(see Proposition
4.8
).
The difference between both choices is the following: on one hand, with
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})
we compute the covariance matrix at
x\in S
with respect to the measure
\nu_{\delta,N}
, that is taking into account the density after correction ; on the other hand, with
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}
we compute the covariance matrix
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\mu_{N})
directly with respect to the empirical measure, not taking into account any density correction, and then we globally multiply by
\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}\right)
in order to obtain the correct density in the limit. Both choices are reasonable, nevertheless
decoupling density and covariance estimation is significantly more adapted to apply concentration inequality as evidenced below in Proposition
5.6
.
We start with investigating the convergence of the deterministic part
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})
,
\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)
towards
\Pi_{T_{x}S}
in Proposition
5.4
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Assume that
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
,
(H_{2})
and
(H_{3})
and let
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
. Then, for
x\in S
such that
T_{x}S
exists (in particular for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a.e.
x\in S
),

(71)

\|\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\leq M\left(\left|\theta_{\delta}(x)-\theta(x)\right|+\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)-\theta(x)\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|\right)\xrightarrow[\delta,r\to 0_{+}]{}0\>,

and

(72)

\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\leq M\left(\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{B(x,r)}\left|\theta_{\delta}-\theta\right|\>d\mu+\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|\right)\>.

Remark 5.5
.

Note that the convergence of
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})
can be inferred provided that
\delta
,
r
tending to
0
additionally satisfy
\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{B(x,r)}\left|\theta_{\delta}-\theta\right|\>d\mu\rightarrow 0
, as done in the proof of Proposition
5.6
.

Proof.

We recall that
\Phi(\theta(x))\theta(x)=1
(see Proposition
4.8
) and
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\mu)
is uniformly bounded by
\frac{C_{0}\|\varphi\|_{\infty}}{C_{\varphi}}
(see (
68
)).
Hence, if there exists an approximate tangent space
T_{x}S
at
x\in S
, we recall that
\|\Phi\|_{\infty}+{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\leq M
thanks to Lemma
4.7
(i)
and thus,

\displaystyle\left\|\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta}(x)\right)\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)-\Phi(\theta(x))\theta(x)\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\Phi(\theta_{\delta}(x))-\Phi(\theta(x))\right|\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\|+|\Phi(\theta(x))|\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)-\theta(x)\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|

\displaystyle\leq M\left(|\theta_{\delta}(x)-\theta(x)|+\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)-\theta(x)\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\right)\xrightarrow[\delta,r\to 0_{+}]{}0\>,

where the convergence to
0
holds thanks to Proposition
3.4
and Proposition
5.3
.
Then, for
x\in S
, recalling that
\|\psi_{r}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\varphi\|_{\infty}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})-\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)\right\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\left\|\int_{B(x,r)}\psi_{r}(y-x)\Phi(\theta_{\delta}(y))\>d\mu(y)-\int_{B(x,r)}\psi_{r}(y-x)\Phi(\theta(y))\>d\mu(y)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\varphi\|_{\infty}}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\int_{B(x,r)}|\theta_{\delta}-\theta|\>d\mu

and we can conclude the proof of Proposition
5.4
by triangular inequality.
∎

We can now infer in Proposition
5.6
the mean convergence of both tangent space estimators
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}
and
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta,N})
.

Proposition 5.6

(Pointwise tangent space estimator)
.

Assume that
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
,
(H_{2})
and
(H_{3})
and let
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
.
Let
\mu_{N}
be the empirical measure associated with
\mu
. Let
\nu_{\delta}
and
\nu_{\delta,N}
be defined as in (
55
).
Then there exists a constant
M\geq 0

such that for all
x\in S
and for all
\delta,r>0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}

(73)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})-\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\right\|\right]\leq\frac{M}{\sqrt{Nr^{d}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)-\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)\right\|\right]\leq M\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{Nr^{d}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}\right)\>,

and furthermore assuming
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\delta,r<1
,

(74)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})-\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})\right\|\right]\leq\frac{M}{\delta}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-1/d}&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-1/2}\ln N&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-1/2}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right.\>.

Moreover,
there exist
(\delta_{N})_{N}
,
(r_{N})_{N}\subset(0,1)^{\mathbb{N}^{\ast}}
both tending to
0
such that for
\mathcal{H}^{d}
–a.e.
x\in S
,

(75)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sigma_{r_{N},\delta_{N},N}(x)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\Sigma_{r_{N}}(x,\nu_{\delta_{N},N})-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

Remark 5.7
.

For the sake of simplicity, let us consider the case
r=\delta
and let us recall that we are interested in the convergence regime that is
N\delta^{d}\to+\infty
so that the rate
(N\delta^{d})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
obtained in (
73
) is faster than the rate
(N\delta^{d})^{-\frac{1}{d}}
obtained in (
74
) as soon as
d>2
.
Note also that the a.e. convergence of
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})
to
\Pi_{T_{x}S}
is proven assuming some relation between
\delta
and
r
tending to
0
while in the case of
\sigma_{r,\delta}
it holds as soon as
\delta
and
r
tend to
0
without further requirement.

Proof.

Let us start with (
73
), similarly to the proof of Proposition
3.5
. By linearity,
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})\right]=\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)
and note that since for
A=(a_{ij})\in{\rm M}_{n}(\mathbb{R})
,
\|A\|^{2}\leq\|A\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{ij}a_{ij}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})-\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\right\|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}{\rm Var}\left(\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})_{ij}\right)=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}^{2}r^{2d}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Var}\left((Z_{1})_{ij}\right)\leq\frac{n^{2}\|\varphi\|_{\infty}}{C_{\varphi}^{2}Nr^{2d}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{\{|X_{1}-x|<r\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n^{2}C_{0}\|\varphi\|_{\infty}^{2}}{C_{\varphi}^{2}Nr^{d}}\>,

where
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{N}
are independent and satisfy

\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}Z_{k}\quad\text{with}\quad Z_{k}=\psi\left(\frac{X_{k}-x}{r}\right)\quad\text{and in particular,}\quad\|Z_{k}\|\leq\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\>.

We obtain

(76)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})-\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\right\|\right]\leq\frac{M}{\sqrt{Nr^{d}}}\>.

Moreover, thanks to the uniform bounds
\|\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\mu)\|\leq M
and
\|\Phi\|_{\infty}+{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\leq M
(see (
68
) and Lemma
4.7
(i)
), we have

\displaystyle\left\|\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)-\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(x)\right)\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})-\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta}(x)\right)\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq M\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})-\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\right\|+\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\|\left|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(x)\right)-\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta}(x)\right)\right|

(77)

\displaystyle\leq M\left(\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})-\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu)\right\|+\left|\theta_{\delta,N}(x)-\theta_{\delta}(x)\right|\right)\>.

We then infer (
73
) thanks to Proposition
3.5
and (
76
) taking the mean value in (
77
) above.

Combining the a.e. convergence (
71
) with (
73
), we infer that the convergence
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\sigma_{r_{N},\delta_{N},N}(x)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0
in (
75
) holds for any sequence
(r_{N})_{N}
,
(\delta_{N})_{N}
tending to
0
and satisfying both
N\delta_{N}^{d}\to+\infty
and
Nr_{N}^{d}\to+\infty
.

We now prove (
74
). First note that in general
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})\right]
is not equal
3
3

3

Actually, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})\right]=\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\int_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{N}}\psi_{r}(x_{1}-x)\Phi\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\frac{1}{C_{\eta}\delta^{d}}\eta\left(\frac{|x_{1}-x_{k}|}{\delta}\right)\right)\>d\mu(x_{1})\ldots d\mu(x_{N})\>.

to
\displaystyle\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})
, and we did not manage to directly obtain concentration as we did previously with
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}
. However,
thanks to the property of
\psi
given in (
67
), we have that for
r>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the function
\displaystyle f:y\mapsto\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\psi\left(\frac{y-x}{r}\right)
satisfies

{\rm Lip}(f)\leq\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\frac{1}{r}\left(\|\varphi\|_{\infty}+{\rm Lip}(\varphi)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\|f\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\quad\text{and}\quad\supp(f)\subset B(x,r)\>,

and we can directly apply Theorem
4.9
(ii)
in the ball
B=B(x,r)
of radius
R_{B}=r<1
, together with the
d
–Ahlfors regularity of
\mu
, to obtain (
74
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})-\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})\right|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\>d\nu_{\delta,N}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\>d\nu_{\delta}\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d+1}}\left(\|\varphi\|_{\infty}+{\rm Lip}(\varphi)\right)\beta_{B(x,r)}(\nu_{\delta,N},\nu_{\delta})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\displaystyle\frac{M}{r^{d+1}}\frac{r}{\delta}\mu(B(x,r))N^{-\frac{1}{d}}&\leq&\displaystyle M\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta}&\text{if}&d>2\\
&&&&\\
\displaystyle\frac{M}{r^{d+1}}\frac{r}{\delta}\mu(B(x,r))N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N&\leq&\displaystyle M\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N}{\delta}&\text{if}&d=2\\
&&&&\\
\displaystyle\frac{M}{r^{d+1}}\frac{r}{\delta}\mu(B(x,r))N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\leq&\displaystyle M\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{2}}}{\delta}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right.\>.

We are left with proving the last past of statement (
75
).
Assuming for instance
d>2
, the case
d\leq 2
does not differ hereafter, and

(\delta_{N})_{N}
,
(r_{N})_{N}
both tend to
0
and
\frac{N^{\frac{-1}{d}}}{\delta_{N}}
tends to
0
as well. We then infer from (
72
) in Proposition
5.4
and (
74
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\Sigma_{r_{N}}(x,\nu_{\delta_{N},N})-\Sigma_{r_{N}}(x,\nu)\right\|\right]\leq M\underbrace{\frac{N^{\frac{-1}{d}}}{\delta_{N}}}_{\longrightarrow 0}+\frac{M}{r_{N}^{d}}\int_{B(x,r_{N})}\left|\theta_{\delta_{N}}-\theta\right|\>d\mu+M\underbrace{\left\|\Sigma_{r_{N}}(x,\nu)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|}_{\longrightarrow 0\text{ by Proposition\penalty\ \ref{propCVSigmar}}}\>.

Finally noting that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{N}^{d}}\int_{B(x,r_{N})}\left|\theta_{\delta_{N}}-\theta\right|\>d\mu\leq\frac{1}{r_{N}^{d}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\theta_{\delta_{N}}-\theta\right|\>d\mu}_{\xrightarrow[\delta_{N}\to 0]{}0}
,
one can adapt
(r_{N})_{N}\to 0
to ensure that this last term converges to
0
as well.
∎

5.2.
A varifold type estimator

Let
0<r,\delta<1
. Building upon the definitions of
\nu_{\delta,N}
(
55
),
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})
(Definition
5.2
) and
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}
(
70
), we can now introduce two varifold-type estimators
W_{r,\delta,N}
and
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
of
V_{S}
: we define the Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{{\rm Sym}}_{+}(n)

(78)

W_{r,\delta,N}=\nu_{\delta,N}\otimes\delta_{\sigma_{r,\delta,N}}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}=\nu_{\delta,N}\otimes\delta_{\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})}

together with their deterministic counterpart:

(79)

W_{r,\delta}=\nu_{\delta}\otimes\delta_{\sigma_{r,\delta}}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}=\nu_{\delta}\otimes\delta_{\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})}\>.

We recall, for instance considering
W_{r,\delta,N}
, that with such a definition (see Section
2.3
) we equivalently have that for all
f\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n))
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{{\rm Sym}}_{+}(n)}f\>dW_{r,\delta,N}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)\right)\>d\nu_{\delta,N}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)\right)\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(x))\>d\mu_{N}(x)\>.

Note that
W_{r,\delta,N}
(respectively
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
,
W_{r,\delta}
,
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}
) are Radon measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
but are not
d
–varifolds, indeed the covariance matrices
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)=\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(x))\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})
(resp.
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})
,
\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)
,
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})
) are generally not orthogonal projectors and can not be identified with elements of
{\rm G}_{d,n}
.
Nevertheless, considering the Radon measure
W_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{\Pi_{T_{x}S}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
rather than the
d
–varifold
V_{S}=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\otimes\delta_{T_{x}S}
, it is possible to show that
W_{r,\delta,N}
is a convergent estimator of
W_{S}
as stated in Proposition
5.8
below. We refer to the last part of Section
2.3
for more details concerning the identification between
W_{S}
and
V_{S}
. We also recall that the localized Bounded Lipschitz distance is introduced in Definition
2.4
, we recall that
\beta_{D}
stands for
\beta_{D\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)}
hereafter.

Proposition 5.8
.

Assume that
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
and
(H_{2})
.
Then,

(1)

case
d>2
and
D
arbitrary bounded open set: there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0},\eta,\varphi)\geq 0
such that for any
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
bounded open set and
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
0<\delta,r<1
satisfying
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\min(\delta,r)
,

\left.\begin{array}[]{r}\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{D}(W_{r,\delta,N},\>W_{r,\delta})\right]\\
\mathbb{E}[\beta_{D}(\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N},\>\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta})]\end{array}\right\}\leq M\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\min(\delta,r)}\mu(D^{\gamma_{N}})\quad\text{with }\gamma_{N}=N^{-\frac{d-2}{d^{2}}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0\>.

(2)

case
B
open ball: there exists a constant
M=M(d,C_{0},\eta,\varphi)\geq 0
such that for any open ball
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
R_{B}<1
, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}
,
0<r,\delta<1
satisfying
N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\leq\min(\delta,r,R_{B})
,

(80)

\left.\begin{array}[]{r}\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(W_{r,\delta,N},\>W_{r,\delta})\right]\\
\mathbb{E}[\beta_{B}(\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N},\>\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta})]\end{array}\right\}\leq\frac{M}{\min(\delta,r)}\mu(B)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right..

Proof.

We recall that
M\geq 0
stands for a generic constant that may vary from one line to another (see Remark
5.1
).
As a first step, we prove the result for
W_{r,\delta,N}
and then we point out the main differences when considering
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
in order to complete the proof. Let
D
be a bounded open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
f\in{\rm C}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)\right)
be such that
{\rm Lip}(f)\leq 1
and
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and assume that
\supp f\subset D\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
.

Step
1
:
we first prove the statement for
W_{r,\delta,N}
.
By triangular inequality and definition (
78
), (
79
) of
W_{r,\delta,N}
and
W_{r,\delta}
,

(81)

\displaystyle\left|\int f\>dW_{r,\delta,N}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\int f\>dW_{r,\delta}\right|\leq A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}\quad\text{with}\quad

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)\right)\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(x))\>(d\mu_{N}(x)-d\mu(x))\>\right|

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)\right)\left(\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(x))-\Phi(\theta_{\delta}(x))\right)\>d\mu(x)\>\right|

\displaystyle A_{3}

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)\right)-f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)\right)\right)\Phi(\theta_{\delta}(x))\>d\mu(x)\>\right|\>.

We will use throughout the proof that
\displaystyle\|\Phi\|_{\infty}+{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\leq M
(see Lemma
4.7
(i)). Applying Proposition
3.5
, we first obtain

(82)

\mathbb{E}\left[A_{2}\right]\leq\|f\|_{\infty}{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\int_{D}\mathbb{E}[|\theta_{\delta,N}-\theta_{\delta}|]\>d\mu\leq\frac{M}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}\mu(D)\>.

We can then apply Proposition
5.6
to infer

(83)

\mathbb{E}\left[A_{3}\right]\leq{\rm Lip}(f)\>\|\Phi\|_{\infty}\int_{D}\mathbb{E}[\left\|\sigma_{r,\delta,N}-\sigma_{r,\delta}\right\|]\>d\mu\leq M\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{Nr^{d}}}\right)\mu(D)\>.

We are left with
A_{1}
, we introduce
g\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
g(x):=f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)\right)\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(x))
and
it remains to check that
g
satisfies assumption (
36
) of Proposition
4.3
.
Let
x
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Recalling Definition
5.2
and (
66
), (
67
), and using
{\rm Lip}(\psi_{r})\leq\frac{1}{r}{\rm Lip}(\psi)
, we infer,

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})-\Sigma_{r}(y,\mu_{N})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\|\psi_{r}(z-x)-\psi_{r}(z-y)\right\|\>d\mu_{N}(z)\leq M\frac{{\rm Lip}(\psi)}{r^{d+1}}\mu_{N}\left(B(x,r)\cup B(y,r)\right)|x-y|

(84)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M}{r}\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)|x-y|\>.

We then recall that by Lemma
4.7

(ii)
:

(85)

\left|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(x)\right)-\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(y)\right)\right|\leq{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\frac{M}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)|x-y|

and consequently, from (
84
) and (
85
) we infer:

\displaystyle\left\|\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(y)\right\|=\left\|\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(x))\Sigma_{r}(x,\mu_{N})-\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(y))\Sigma_{r}(y,\mu_{N})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Phi\|_{\infty}\frac{M}{r}\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)|x-y|+\|\Sigma_{r}(y,\mu_{N})\|\frac{M}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)|x-y|

(86)

\displaystyle\leq M\left(\frac{1}{r}\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)+\frac{1}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)\right)|x-y|\>,

where we used
\displaystyle\|\Sigma_{r}(y,\mu_{N})\|\leq\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\int_{B(y,r)}\|\psi_{r}(z-y)\|\>d\mu_{N}(z)\leq M\frac{\mu_{N}(B(y,r))}{r^{d}}\leq M\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)
. Therefore coming back to
g
, we have thanks (
85
) and (
86
),

\displaystyle|g(x)

\displaystyle-g(y)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|f(x,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x))|\left|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(x)\right)-\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(y)\right)\right|+\left|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(y)\right)\right||f(x,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(x))-f(y,\sigma_{r,\delta,N}(y))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\infty}{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\frac{M}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)|x-y|+\|\Phi\|_{\infty}{\rm Lip}(f)\big(1+{\rm Lip}(\sigma_{r,\delta,N})\big)|x-y|

(87)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)+\frac{1}{r}\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)+\frac{1}{\delta}\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)+1\right)|x-y|

and
g
therefore satisfies assumption (
36
) of Proposition
4.3
with
\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{3}=\kappa_{0}(=M)
and
\kappa=1
. We can thus apply Proposition
4.3
(i)
in order to control
\mathbb{E}[|A_{1}|]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int g\>d\mu_{N}-\int g\>d\mu\right|\right]
and we can then conclude the proof of
(i)
thanks to (
81
), (
82
) and (
83
), noting that by assumption
N\delta^{d}\geq 1
and thus
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}=(N\delta^{d})^{-1/2}\leq(N\delta^{d})^{-1/d}=\frac{N^{-1/d}}{\delta}
if
d>2
and therefore

\mathbb{E}[A_{2}]
are
\mathbb{E}[A_{3}]
are controlled by
\displaystyle M\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\min(\delta,r)}\mu(D^{\gamma_{N}})
as well.

Step
2
:
Starting from the similar decomposition (
81
) for
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
, i.e. replacing
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}
,
\sigma_{r,\delta}
with

\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta,N})
,
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta})
, we follow the same strategy as in Step
1
.
Applying Proposition
3.5
and Proposition
5.6
, we similarly obtain

(88)

\mathbb{E}\left[A_{2}\right]\leq\frac{M}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}\mu(D)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\left[A_{3}\right]\leq\frac{M}{\delta}\mu(D)\times\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right.\>.

We are left with
A_{1}
, we similarly introduce
g\in{\rm C}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
s.t. for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
g(x):=f(x,\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N}))\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(x))
and
it remains to check that
g
satisfies assumption (
36
) of Proposition
4.3
.
Let
x
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Similarly to (
84
) and additionally using
|\Phi(\theta_{\delta,N}(z))|\leq M
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N})-\Sigma_{r}(y,\nu_{\delta,N})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C_{\varphi}r^{d}}\int_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\|\psi_{r}(z-x)-\psi_{r}(z-y)\right\|\left|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(z)\right)\right|\>d\mu_{N}(z)

(89)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{M}{r}\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)|y-x|\>.

Coming back to
g
, we have thanks to (
85
) and (
89
),

\displaystyle|g(x)

\displaystyle-g(y)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|f(x,\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N}))|\left|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(x)\right)-\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(y)\right)\right|+\left|\Phi\left(\theta_{\delta,N}(y)\right)\right||f(x,\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta,N}))-f(y,\Sigma_{r}(y,\nu_{\delta,N}))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\infty}\frac{M}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)|y-x|+\|\Phi\|_{\infty}{\rm Lip}(f)\big(1+{\rm Lip}(\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta,N}))\big)|x-y|

(90)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle M\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\Delta_{\delta,N}(x,y)+\frac{1}{r}\Delta_{r,N}(x,y)+1\right)|x-y|

and
g
again satisfies assumption (
36
) of Proposition
4.3
with
\displaystyle\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}=\kappa_{0}
,
\kappa_{3}=0
and
\kappa=1
and applying Proposition
4.3
allows to control
\mathbb{E}[|A_{1}|]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\int g\>d\mu_{N}-\int g\>d\mu\right|\right]
and conclude the proof of
(i)
thanks to (
88
) and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}\leq\frac{N^{-\frac{1}{d}}}{\delta}
for
d>2
.

Step
3
:
Assuming moreover that
D=B
is an open ball of radius
0<R_{B}<1
, we have
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq R_{B}
since
\supp f\subset B\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
and
{\rm Lip}(f)\leq 1
and thus
\|g\|_{\infty}\leq MR_{B}=\kappa\kappa_{0}
now with
\kappa=R_{B}
while
\kappa_{0}=M
is unchanged. Therefore
(ii)
follows similarly from (

), (

) and Proposition
4.3
(ii)
, also noting that for

,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N\delta^{d}}}=\frac{N^{-1/2}}{\delta^{1/2}}\leq\frac{N^{-1/2}}{\delta}
and therefore

\mathbb{E}[A_{2}]
and
\mathbb{E}[A_{3}]
are controlled by the right hand side of (
80
) in both cases, hence concluding the proof of Proposition
5.8
.
∎

In order to infer the convergence of the varifold-type estimators
W_{r,\delta,N}
and
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
towards
W
, we are left with investigating the convergence of the deterministic part, which is exactly the purpose of Proposition
5.9
.

Proposition 5.9
.

Assume that
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
,
(H_{2})
and
(H_{3})
and let
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. Then

(91)

\beta_{D}\left(W_{r,\delta},W_{S}\right)\xrightarrow[\delta,r\to 0]{}0\quad\text{and}\quad\beta_{D}\left(\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta},W_{S}\right)\xrightarrow[\delta,r\to 0]{}0\>.

Remark 5.10
.

Note that in the proof of Proposition
5.9
, we establish the following bounds that will be of use in the sequel:

(92)

\beta_{D}\left(W_{r,\delta},W_{S}\right)\leq M\int_{D}|\theta_{\delta}-\theta|\>d\mu+M\int_{D}\|\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\>d\mu(x)\>,

and

(93)

\left.\begin{array}[]{r}\beta_{D}\left(\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta},W_{S}\right)\\
\displaystyle\int_{D}\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|\>d\nu(x)\end{array}\right\}\leq M\int_{D^{r}}|\theta_{\delta}-\theta|\>d\mu+M\int_{D}\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\>d\mu(x)\>.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
5.8
, we first deal with the case of
W_{r,\delta}
(Step
1
) and then we check that a similar strategy is valid when handling
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}
(Step
2
).
Let
D
be an open set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
f\in{\rm C}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)\right)
satisfying
{\rm Lip}(f)\leq 1
and
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and assume that
\supp f\subset D\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
. We recall (and we use hereafter) that
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
and
\mu
are equivalent:
\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\leq\theta_{max}\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
and
\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}=\theta^{-1}\mu\leq\theta_{min}^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
.

Step
1
:
let us write

(94)

\left|\int f\>dW_{r,\delta}-\int f\>dW_{S}\right|\leq A_{1}+A_{2}\quad\text{with}\quad\\
A_{1}=\left|\int_{D}f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)\right)\left(d\nu_{\delta}(x)-d\nu(x)\right)\right|\quad\text{and}\quad A_{2}=\left|\int_{D}\left(f\left(x,\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)\right)-f\left(x,\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right)\right)\>d\nu(x)\right|\>.

Starting with
A_{1}
, we have

(95)

A_{1}=\left|\int_{D}f(x,\sigma_{r,\delta}(x))\left(\Phi(\theta_{\delta}(x))-\Phi(\theta(x))\right)d\mu(x)\right|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}{\rm Lip}(\Phi)\int_{D}\left|\theta_{\delta}-\theta\right|\>d\mu\>

while

(96)

A_{2}\leq{\rm Lip}(f)\int_{D}\|\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\>d\nu(x)\>.

Therefore, combining (
94
), (
95
) and (
96
) we obtain (
92
), and then recalling (
71
) (in Proposition
5.4
) and Proposition
3.4
, we conclude by dominated convergence that

\beta_{D}\left(W_{r,\delta},W_{S}\right)\leq M\int_{D\cap S}|\theta_{\delta}(x)-\theta(x)|+\|\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\>d\mathcal{H}^{d}(x)\xrightarrow[\delta,r\to 0_{+}]{}0\>,

where the domination holds with a constant: for
x\in S
,
|\theta_{\delta}(x)|\leq M
thanks to (
60
) and
\|\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)\|\leq\|\Phi\|_{\infty}M
thanks to (
68
).

Step
2
:
we similarly write

(97)

\left|\int f\>d\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}-\int f\>dW_{S}\right|\leq A_{1}+A_{2}\quad\text{with}\quad\\
A_{1}=\left|\int_{D}f\left(x,\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})\right)\left(d\nu_{\delta}(x)-d\nu(x)\right)\right|\quad\text{and}\quad A_{2}=\left|\int_{D}\left(f\left(x,\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})\right)-f\left(x,\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right)\right)\>d\nu(x)\right|\>.

We obtain the same control as in (
95
) for
A_{1}
:

(98)

A_{1}=\left|\int_{D}f(x,\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta}))\left(\Phi(\theta_{\delta}(x))-\Phi(\theta(x))\right)d\mu(x)\right|\leq M\int_{D}\left|\theta_{\delta}-\theta\right|\>d\mu\>.

On the other hand, recalling that
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
is
d
–Ahlfors regular and since
B(x,r)\subset D^{r}
for
x\in D
, Fubini theorem yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{x\in D}\left(\int_{B(x,r)}|\theta_{\delta}-\theta|\>d\mu\right)\>d\nu(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{y\in D^{r}}|\theta_{\delta}(y)-\theta(y)|\int_{x\in B(y,r)\cap D}\>d\nu(x)\>d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{r^{d}}\int_{y\in D^{r}}|\theta_{\delta}(y)-\theta(y)|\nu(B(y,r))\>d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq M\int_{D^{r}}|\theta_{\delta}-\theta|\>d\mu\>,

and then recalling (
72
), we have

(99)

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\rm Lip}(f)\int_{D}\left\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu_{\delta})-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\right\|\>d\nu(x)\leq M\int_{D^{r}}|\theta_{\delta}-\theta|\>d\mu+\int_{D}\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\>d\nu\>.

We obtain from (
97
), (
98
) and (
99
):

\beta_{D}\left(\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta},W_{S}\right)\leq M\int_{D^{r}}|\theta_{\delta}-\theta|\>d\mu+M\int_{D}\|\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)-\Pi_{T_{x}S}\|\>d\mu(x)\>,

whence together with (
99
) we obtain (
93
) as well.
Moreover, for any
r\leq 1
,
D^{r}\subset D^{1}

and we similarly conclude by dominated convergence applied together with Proposition
3.4
and Proposition
5.3
that the convergence holds for
\delta,r\rightarrow 0
.
∎

Note that the convergence (
91
) holds as soon as
\delta
and
r
tend to
0
for both
W_{r,\delta}
and
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}
, while the pointwise convergence of
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta})
additionally requires some relation between
\delta
and
r
(see Remark
5.5
). We combine Proposition
5.8
and Proposition
5.9
to state the following convergence result for
W_{r,\delta,N}
and
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
. Due to the lack of explicit rate in the regularity class
\mathcal{Q}
concerning the convergence of the deterministic part stated in Proposition
5.9
, the mean convergence stated in Corollary
5.11
suffers the same lack of explicit rate. Such an issue is then addressed in Section
6
and
7
assuming a stronger regularity model
\mathcal{P}
defined in (
100
).

Corollary 5.11
.

Assume that
S
and
\theta
satisfy
(H_{1})
,
(H_{2})
and
(H_{3})
.
Let
B
be an open ball of radius
0<R_{B}<1
and let
(\delta_{N})_{N},\>(r_{N})_{N}\subset(0,1)^{\mathbb{N}^{\ast}}
be sequences tending to
0
and satisfying

\frac{1}{\min(\delta_{N},r_{N})}\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}N^{-\frac{1}{d}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0&\text{if}&d>2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ln N\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0&\text{if}&d=2\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0&\text{if}&d<2\end{array}\right.\>.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(W_{r_{N},\delta_{N},N},W_{S})\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0
and
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\beta_{B}(\widetilde{W}_{r_{N},\delta_{N},N},W_{S})\right]\xrightarrow[N\to+\infty]{}0
.

6.
Quantitative estimates in a piecewise Hölder regularity class

In Proposition
3.4
and Proposition
4.8
, as well as in Proposition
5.3
and
5.9
the convergence of
\theta_{\delta}
to
\theta
,
\Sigma_{r}
/
\sigma_{r,\delta}
to
\Pi_{T_{x}S}
,
\nu_{\delta}
to
\nu=\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}
and
W_{r,\delta}
/
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}
to
W_{S}
do not come with any convergence rate, which also impacts the convergence results of the associated estimators stated in Corollary
3.6
, in Corollary
4.10
, in Proposition
5.6
and in Corollary
5.11
.
The current section is then dedicated to quantifying the aforementioned deterministic convergences (of
\theta_{\delta}
,
\nu_{\delta}
,
\Sigma_{r}
/
\sigma_{r,\delta}
,
W_{r,\delta}
/
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}
) under stronger regularity assumptions both for the set
S
and the density
\theta
.

In Section
6.1
, we introduce the regularity framework that we implement in the sequel and is referred to as assumptions
(H_{1})
to (

H
7

) in statements and called
piecewise Hölder regularity class

\mathcal{P}
, see (
100
).
Loosely speaking, we assume that the set
S
is at least uniformly piecewise
{\rm C}^{1,a}
(see Definition
6.2
) and the density
\theta
is similarly uniformly piecewise
{\rm C}^{0,b}
(see Definition
6.5
): such regularity assumptions allow for a singular set
\mathfrak{S}=S_{sg}\cup\Theta_{sg}
that is a union of Ahlfors regular sets of lower dimensions.
With such regularity notions at hand, we first quantify in Section
6.2
the pointwise convergence of
\theta_{\delta}
to
\theta
and
\Sigma_{r}(x,\nu)
/
\sigma_{r,\delta}(x)
to
\Pi_{T_{x}S}
, see Proposition
6.11
. In Section
6.3
, we subsequently provide similar rates for the Bounded Lipschitz distances between the measures
\nu_{\delta}
,
\nu
(in Proposition
6.12
) and
W_{r,\delta}
,
W_{S}
/
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta}
,
W_{S}
(in Proposition
6.13
). In Section
6.4
, we propose to replace
W_{r,\delta,N}
,
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
, that are measures in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times{\rm Sym}_{+}(n)
with varifolds
V_{r,\delta,N}
,
\widetilde{V}_{r,\delta,N}
: to this end, we substitute the tangent plane estimators
\sigma_{r,\delta,N}
and
\Sigma_{r}(\cdot,\nu_{\delta,N})
with an orthogonal projector of rank
d
thanks to a classical (at least from a numerical perspective) truncation process in the eigen decomposition. We conclude Section
6
with Theorem
6.15
that states the mean convergence of
W_{r,\delta,N}
,
\widetilde{W}_{r,\delta,N}
and
V_{r,\delta,N}
,
\widetilde{V}_{r,\delta,N}
to
W_{S}
consistently with the rates established for the deterministic part in Proposition
6.13
.

6.1.
Piecewise Hölder regularity class

Let us recall that up to this point, we only assumed
{\rm L}^{1}
–regularity (with respect to
\nu
) for the density
\theta
. It is well-known that such an hypothesis is sufficient to obtain the pointwise convergence of averages
\oint_{B(x,\delta)}\theta\>d\nu
at
\nu
–a.e. point
x
, which is somehow connected to the pointwise convergence of
\theta_{\delta}
to
\theta
. However, it is not possible to quantify such a convergence in terms of
\delta
only assuming
{\rm L}^{1}
–regularity of
\theta
, let us recall the following example:

Example 6.1
.

Let us illustrate that assuming
f\in{\rm L}^{1}
is not enough to provide a uniform bound on the pointwise convergence of the averages on small balls.
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
k\neq 0
, we consider the
{\rm L}^{1}
–function
f_{k}:(0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

f_{k}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}1&\text{if }\lfloor kx\rfloor\text{ is even}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\>.

The function
f_{k}
is
\frac{2}{k}
–periodic and “equally” oscillates between
0
and
1
. Then, the average
\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{B(x,\delta)}f_{k}(y)\>dy

is close to
f_{k}(x)\in\{0,1\}
if
\delta\ll\frac{1}{k}
is small with respect to the period, whereas it remains close to
\frac{1}{2}
(whence far from
f_{k}(x)
) as long as
\delta\gg\frac{1}{k}
. It illustrates that quantifying (in terms of
\delta
and uniformly with respect to
f_{k}
and thus
k
) the almost everywhere pointwise convergence of
\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{B(\cdot,\delta)}f_{k}(y)\>dy
to
f_{k}
requires to restrict to a class of functions with stronger regularity: the
{\rm L}^{1}
–assumption does not provide enough control over the oscillation of the function.

We then consider a stronger regularity framework both for
S
(see Definition
6.2
) and
\theta
(see Definition
6.5
), concretely adding assumptions (

H
4

) to (

H
7

) to the current regularity framework consisting of assumptions
(H_{1})
to
(H_{3})
. As already introduced in Section
1.1
, we then obtain the regularity class

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle=\{\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\>:\>S,\>\theta\text{ satisfy \hyperref@@ii[hypH1]{$(H_{1})$} \text{ to } \eqref{hypH7}}\}

(100)

\displaystyle=\{\mu=\theta\mathcal{H}^{d}_{|S}\>:\>S,\>\theta\text{ are respectively uniformly piecewise }{\rm C}^{1,a},\;{\rm C}^{0,b}\text{ according to Definitions\penalty\ \ref{dfnPwHolderS},\penalty\ \ref{dfnPwHolderTheta}}\}

that depends on
d,\widetilde{C_{0}},\eta,\varphi,\theta_{min/max},C=\max(C_{\theta,sg},C_{S,sg}),R=\min(R_{\theta,sg},R_{S,sg})
.
We mention that the following Definitions
6.2
and
6.5
are close though different (see in particular (

H
5

)) from Definition 7.2 in
[
BLM17
]
.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
0<a\leq 1
and let
S
be a closed set satisfying
\mathcal{H}^{d}(S)<\infty
. We say that
S
is uniformly piecewise
{\rm C}^{1,a}
if there exist
a closed set
S_{sg}\subset S
,
C=C_{S,sg}\geq 1
and
